\widetilde{\pi}\,:\,C(G\wr_{*}S_{N}^{+})\rightarrow A
such that

({\rm id}\otimes\widetilde{\pi})\rho=\pi\text{ and }\widetilde{\pi}|_{C(S_{N}^{+})}=\iota.

Proof.

The uniqueness being obvious, let us show the existence. Write
\pi(a)=\sum_{i}e_{i}\otimes\pi_{i}(a)
and note that, since
\pi
is a unital
*
-homomorphism, each
\pi_{i}\,:\,C(G)\rightarrow A
is a unital
*
-homomorphism. Define
\pi_{0}\,:\,C(G)^{*N}*C(S_{N}^{+})\rightarrow A
by
\pi_{0}\circ\nu_{i}:=\pi_{i}
and
\pi_{0}|_{C(S_{N}^{+})}:=\iota
. Note that the commutations relations
[\pi(a),({\rm id}\otimes\iota)(\beta(b))]=0
imply that, for all
a\in C(G)
and all
1\leq i,j\leq N
one has
\pi_{0}(\nu_{i}(a)u_{ij}-u_{ij}\nu_{i}(a))=0
. Hence
I\subseteq\ker(\pi_{0})
which implies that there exists a unital
*
-homomorphism
\widetilde{\pi}\,:\,C(G\wr_{*}S_{N}^{+})\rightarrow A
satisfying the properties of the Proposition.∎

Let now
G,H
be any compact quantum groups and
\beta\,:\,B\rightarrow B\otimes C(H)
be any action of
H
on the unital C*-algebra
B
. Motivated by Proposition
3.1
we want to define the C*-algebra
C(\mathbb{G})
of the generalized free wreath product
\mathbb{G}=G\wr_{*,\beta}H
as the universal unital C*-algebra generated by
C(H)
and the coefficients of a unital
*
-homomorphism
\rho\,:\,C(G)\rightarrow B\otimes C(\mathbb{G})
with the relation
[\rho(a),\beta(b)]=0
for all
a\in C(G)
and
b\in B
. It is not clear however that such a C*-algebra even exists and we will elaborate on that in the next subsection.

3.2.
The amalgamated free wreath product quantum group

For the remainder of this section, we fix an action
\beta\,:\,B\rightarrow B\otimes C(H)
of a CQG
H
on finite dimensional C*-algebra
B
and a CQG
G
with a dual quantum subgroup
F
,
C(F)\hookrightarrow C(G)
. We denote by
E_{F}:C(G)\rightarrow C(F)
the canonical unital ucp map defined in Proposition
2.5
.

Recall that the direct product quantum group
H\times F
is defined as the compact quantum group such that
C(H\times F)=C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(F)
and
\Delta_{H\times F}=\Sigma_{23}(\Delta_{H}\otimes\Delta_{F})
. Note that, by universal property of the maximal tensor product,
C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(F)
is the universal unital C*-algebra generated
C(H)
and
C(F)
with the relations making
C(H)
and
C(F)
commuting.

We now define
the amalgamated free wreath product quantum group
C*-algebra
C(\mathbb{G})
as the universal unital C*-algebra generated by
C(H)
,
C(F)
and the coefficients of a unital
*
-homomorphism
\rho\,:\,C(G)\rightarrow B\otimes C(\mathbb{G})
with the relations
[\rho(a),\beta(b)]=0
for all
a\in C(G)
and
b\in B
,
C(H)
and
C(F)
commute in
C(\mathbb{G})
and
\rho(f)=1_{B}\otimes f
for all
f\in C(F)\subset C(\mathbb{G})
. More precisely the C*-algebra
C(\mathbb{G})
, if it exists, satisfies the following properties:

•

There exists three unital
*
-homomorphisms
\iota_{H}\,:\,C(H)\rightarrow C(\mathbb{G})
,
\iota_{F}\,:\,C(F)\rightarrow C(\mathbb{G})
and
\rho\,:\,C(G)\rightarrow B\otimes C(\mathbb{G})
such that:

–

[\iota_{H}(x),\iota_{F}(f)]=0
for all
x\in C(H)
and all
f\in C(F)
.

–

[({\rm id}\otimes\iota_{H})(\beta(b)),\rho(a)]=0
for all
a\in C(G)
and all
b\in B
.

–

\rho(f)=1_{B}\otimes\iota_{F}(f)
for all
f\in C(F)
.

•

If
C
is any unital C*-algebra with unital
*
-homomorphisms
\iota^{\prime}_{H}\,:\,C(H)\rightarrow C
,
\iota^{\prime}_{F}\,:\,C(F)\rightarrow C
and
\rho^{\prime}\,:\,C(G)\rightarrow B\otimes C
such that:

–

[\iota^{\prime}_{H}(x),\iota^{\prime}_{F}(f)]=0
for all
x\in C(H)
,
f\in C(F)
,

–

[({\rm id}\otimes\iota^{\prime}_{H})(\beta(b)),\rho^{\prime}(a)]=0
for all
a\in C(G)
,
b\in B
,

–

\rho^{\prime}(f)=1_{B}\otimes\iota^{\prime}_{F}(f)
for all
f\in C(F)
,

then, there exists a unique unital
*
-homomorphism
\nu\,:\,C(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow C
such that:

\nu\circ\iota_{H}=\iota^{\prime}_{H},\,\,\nu\circ\iota_{F}=\iota^{\prime}_{F}\text{ and }({\rm id}\otimes\nu)\circ\rho=\rho^{\prime}.

While it is not clear that
C(\mathbb{G})
even exists, its uniqueness, up to a canonical isomorphism, is an obvious consequence of the universal property defining
C(\mathbb{G})
. When
C(\mathbb{G})
exists, we will sometimes write
\iota\,:\,C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(F)\rightarrow C(\mathbb{G})
the unique unital
*
-homomorphism such that
\iota(x\otimes f)=\iota_{H}(x)\iota_{F}(f)
for all
x\in C(H)
and all
f\in C(F)
.

Remark 3.2
.

Suppose that
C(\mathbb{G})
exists then
\iota
and
\rho
are faithful (in particular, both
\iota_{H}
and
\iota_{F}
are faithful). Indeed, it follows from the universal property, taking
C=C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(G)
,
\iota^{\prime}_{H}(x)=x\otimes 1_{C(F)}
,
\iota^{\prime}_{F}(f)=1_{C(H)}\otimes f
and
\rho^{\prime}(a)=1_{B}\otimes 1_{C(H)}\otimes a
, that there exists a unique unital
*
-homomorphism
\pi\,:\,C(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(G)
such that

({\rm id}\otimes\pi)\rho(a)=1_{B}\otimes 1_{C(H)}\otimes a\text{ for all }a\in C(G)\text{ and }\pi\circ\iota={\rm id}.

We easily deduce that
\iota
and
\rho
are faithful.

Example 3.3
.

When
F
equals
G
i.e. the inclusion
C(F)\subset C(G)
is surjective then
C(\mathbb{G})
exists: it is easy to see that
C(\mathbb{G}):=C(H\times G)=C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(G)
with
\iota_{H}(x)=x\otimes 1_{C(G)}
,
\iota_{F}(a)=1_{C(H)}\otimes a
and
\rho(a)=1_{B}\otimes 1_{C(H)}\otimes a
for all
x\in C(H)
and
a\in C(G)
satisfy the universal property.

We discuss the obvious limitations of such a construction and some of its properties in the following Proposition.

Proposition 3.4
.

The following holds.

(1)

If
B
is finite dimensional then
C(\mathbb{G})
exists.

(2)

If
F
is trivial,
G
is non-trivial and
B
is abelian then
C(\mathbb{G})
exists if and only if
B
is finite dimensional.

(3)

If
C(\mathbb{G})
exists then
C(\mathbb{G})
is generated, as a C*-algebra, by

\iota_{H}(C(H))\cup\{(\omega\otimes{\rm id})(\rho(a))\,:\,a\in C(G),\,\omega\in B^{*}\}.

Proof.

(1)
. If
B
is finite dimensional, it is known
[
FMP24
]
that the universal quantum homomorphism
\rho\,:\,C(G)\rightarrow B\otimes\mathcal{U}
exists. Then, the C*-algebra

C(\mathbb{G}):=\mathcal{U}*(C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(F))/I,

where
I
is the closed two sided ideal generated by

\{(\omega\otimes{\rm id})(\rho(a)\beta(b)-\beta(b)\rho(a))\,:\,\omega\in B^{*},\,a\in C(G),\,b\in B\}

and by
\{(\omega\otimes{\rm id})(\rho(f)-1_{B}\otimes f)\,:\,\omega\in B^{*},\,f\in C(F)\}
does the job.

(2)
. Suppose that
C(\mathbb{G})
exists and define
\mathcal{U}\subset C(\mathbb{G})
to be the C*-subalgebra of
C(\mathbb{G})
generated by
\{(\omega\otimes{\rm id})(\rho(a))\,:\,a\in C(G),\,\omega\in B^{*}\}
so that
\rho(C(G))\subset B\otimes\mathcal{U}
. We show below that
\rho\,:\,C(G)\rightarrow B\otimes\mathcal{U}
is the universal quantum homomorphism from
C(G)
to
B
in the sense of
[
FMP24
]
. Since
{\rm dim}(C(G))\geq 2

[
FMP24
, Theorem D]
will imply that
B
is finite dimensional. Let
\pi\,:\,C(G)\rightarrow B\otimes C
be a unital
*
-homomorphism and define
\iota^{\prime}_{H}:=\varepsilon_{H}(\cdot)1_{C}\,:\,C(H)\rightarrow C
. Note that
({\rm id}\otimes\iota^{\prime}_{H})(\beta(b))=b\otimes 1_{C}
and since
B
is abelian we have
[({\rm id}\otimes\iota^{\prime}_{H})(\beta(b)),\pi(a)]=0
for all
a\in C(G)
and
b\in B
. It follows that there exists a unique unital
*
-homomorphism
\nu\,:\,C(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow C
such that
({\rm id}\otimes\nu)\circ\rho=\pi
(and
\nu\circ\iota_{H}=\iota^{\prime}_{H}
). Defining
\widetilde{\pi}:=\nu|_{\mathcal{U}}
we have
({\rm id}\otimes\widetilde{\pi})\rho=\pi
and the uniqueness of
\widetilde{\pi}
is clear.

(3)
. It is a direct consequence of the uniqueness in the universal property.∎

Motivated by the previous Proposition, we assume for the rest of this paper that
B
is finite dimensional so that
C(\mathbb{G})
exists. From Remark
3.2
we may and will view
C(H),C(F)\subset C(\mathbb{G})
with
\iota_{H}
,
\iota_{F}
being the inclusions. Also, since the map
\iota\,:\,C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(F)\rightarrow C(\mathbb{G})
is faithful we view
C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(F)=\overline{{\rm Span}}(C(H)C(F))\subset C(\mathbb{G})
. Hence, the relations become
[\rho(a),\beta(b)]=0
,
[x,f]=0
and
\rho(f)=1_{B}\otimes f
for all
a\in C(G)
,
b\in B
,
x\in C(H)
and
f\in C(F)
. Note that there is a unique unital
*
-homomorphism
\beta_{G}\,:\,C(G)\otimes B\rightarrow B\otimes C(\mathbb{G})
satisfying
\beta_{G}(a\otimes b)=\rho(a)\beta(b)
, for all
a\in C(G)
,
b\in B
.

3.2.1.
Comultiplication

In the sequel, we assume that
\beta
is a
\psi
-preserving action of
H
on the finite dimensional C*-algebra
B
, where
\psi\in B^{*}
is a faithful state and we denote by
u\in\mathcal{L}(B)\otimes C(H)\subset\mathcal{L}(B)\otimes C(\mathbb{G})
the unitary representation of
H
associated to the action
\beta\,:\,B\rightarrow B\otimes C(H)
as explained before Lemma
2.7
.

Theorem 3.5
.

There exists a unique unital
*
-homomorphism
\Delta\,:\,C(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow C(\mathbb{G})\otimes C(\mathbb{G})
such that
\Delta|_{C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(F)}=\Delta_{H\times F}
and
({\rm id}\otimes\Delta)\rho=(\beta_{G}\otimes{\rm id})({\rm id}\otimes\rho)\Delta_{G}
. Moreover,

(1)

(C(\mathbb{G}),\Delta)
is a CQG and
C(H\times F)=C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(F)\subset C(\mathbb{G})
is a dual quantum subgroup.

(2)

The counit of
\mathbb{G}
is the unique character of
C(\mathbb{G})
satisfying

({\rm id}\otimes\varepsilon_{\mathbb{G}})\rho=\varepsilon_{G}(\cdot)1_{B}\text{ and }\varepsilon_{\mathbb{G}}|_{C(H\times F)}=\varepsilon_{H\times F}.

(3)

If
v\in\mathcal{L}(H_{v})\otimes C(G)
is a f.d. unitary representation of
G
then,

a(v):=({\rm id}\otimes(L_{B}\otimes{\rm id})\rho)(v)u_{23}\in\mathcal{L}(H_{v}\otimes B)\otimes C(\mathbb{G})

is a unitary representation of
\mathbb{G}
on
H_{v}\otimes B
and
\overline{a(v)}\simeq a(\overline{v})
.

(4)

If
v\in\mathcal{L}(H_{v})\otimes C(F)
is a f.d. representation of
F
, seen as a representation of
G
by the fact that
\mathrm{Rep}(F)
is a full subcategory of
\mathrm{Rep}(G)
, then the representation
a(v)
is the representation
v\otimes u\in\mathcal{L}(H_{v}\otimes B)\otimes C(H\times F),
seen as a representation of
C(\mathbb{G})
because we can see
\mathrm{Rep}(H\times F)
as a full subcategory of
\mathrm{Rep}(\mathbb{G})
.

(5)

Every irreducible representation of
\mathbb{G}
is equivalent to a subrepresentation of a tensor product of representations of the form
a(v)
, for
v\in{\rm Irr}(G)
, and irreducible representations of
H
. If the action
\beta
is faithful, any irreducible of
G
is equivalent to a subrepresentation of a tensor product of
a(v)
, for
v\in{\rm Irr}(G)
.

(6)

For all
v,w,t\in\mathrm{Rep}(G)
and all
S\in\mathrm{Mor}_{G}(v\otimes w,t)
, the map

(S\otimes m)\Sigma_{23}\,:\,H_{v}\otimes B\otimes H_{w}\otimes B\rightarrow H_{t}\otimes B

is in
\mathrm{Mor}_{\mathbb{G}}(a(v)\otimes a(w),a(t))
, where
\Sigma_{23}:H_{v}\otimes B\otimes H_{w}\otimes B\rightarrow H_{v}\otimes H_{w}\otimes B\otimes B
is the flip map.

Proof.

Let
\iota^{\prime}:=\Delta_{H\times F}\,:\,C(H\times F)\rightarrow C(\mathbb{G})\otimes C(\mathbb{G})
, from which we get
\iota^{\prime}_{H}=\Delta_{H}
and
\iota^{\prime}_{F}=\Delta_{F}
with commuting images and
\rho^{\prime}:=(\beta_{G}\otimes{\rm id})({\rm id}\otimes\rho)\Delta_{G}\,:\,C(G)\rightarrow B\otimes C(\mathbb{G})\otimes C(\mathbb{G})
. For
a\in{\rm Pol}(G)
one has
\rho^{\prime}(a)=\sum(\beta_{G}\otimes{\rm id})(a_{(1)}\otimes\rho(a_{(2)}))=\sum(\rho(a_{(1)})\otimes 1)(\beta\otimes{\rm id})(\rho(a_{(2)}))
, where
\Delta_{G}(a)=\sum a_{(1)}\otimes a_{(2)}
. Hence,

\displaystyle\rho^{\prime}(a)({\rm id}\otimes\iota^{\prime}_{H})(\beta(b))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho^{\prime}(a)({\rm id}\otimes\Delta_{H})(\beta(b))=\rho^{\prime}(a)(\beta\otimes{\rm id})(\beta(b))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum(\rho(a_{(1)})\otimes 1)(\beta\otimes{\rm id})(\rho(a_{(2)}))(\beta\otimes{\rm id})(\beta(b))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum(\rho(a_{(1)})\otimes 1)(\beta\otimes{\rm id})(\rho(a_{(2)})\beta(b))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum(\rho(a_{(1)})\otimes 1)(\beta\otimes{\rm id})(\beta(b))(\beta\otimes{\rm id})(\rho(a_{(2)}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\beta\otimes{\rm id})(\beta(b))\sum(\rho(a_{(1)})\otimes 1)(\beta\otimes{\rm id})(\rho(a_{(2)}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\rm id}\otimes\iota^{\prime}_{H})(\beta(b))\rho^{\prime}(a).

We also have that for
f\in{\rm Pol}(F)
,
\Delta_{G}(f)=\Delta_{F}(f)=\sum f_{(1)}\otimes f_{(2)}\in{\rm Pol}(F)\otimes{\rm Pol}(F)
and:

\displaystyle\rho^{\prime}(f)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\beta_{G}\otimes{\rm id})({\rm id}\otimes\rho)\Delta_{G}(f)=\sum(\beta_{G}\otimes{\rm id})\left(f_{(1)}\otimes\rho(f_{(2)})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum(\beta_{G}\otimes{\rm id})(f_{(1)}\otimes 1_{B}\otimes f_{(2)})=\sum\rho(f_{(1)})\otimes f_{(2)}=\sum 1_{B}\otimes f_{(1)}\otimes f_{(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1_{B}\otimes\Delta_{F}(f)=1_{B}\otimes\iota^{\prime}_{F}(f).

The existence of
\Delta\,:\,C(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow C(\mathbb{G})\otimes C(\mathbb{G})
now follows from the universal property.

Let us show that
({\rm id}_{\mathcal{L}(H_{v}\otimes B)}\otimes\Delta)(a(v))=a(v)_{12}a(v)_{13}

\forall v\in\mathrm{Rep}(G)
.

\displaystyle({\rm id}\otimes\Delta)[({\rm id}\otimes(L_{B}\otimes{\rm id})\rho)(v)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\rm id}_{\mathcal{L}(H_{v})}\otimes L_{B}\otimes{\rm id}_{C(\mathbb{G})}\otimes{\rm id}_{C(\mathbb{G})})({\rm id}_{\mathcal{L}(H_{v})}\otimes({\rm id}_{B}\otimes\Delta)\rho)(v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\rm id}\otimes L_{B}\otimes{\rm id}\otimes{\rm id})({\rm id}\otimes\beta_{G}\otimes{\rm id})({\rm id}\otimes{\rm id}\otimes\rho)({\rm id}\otimes\Delta_{G})(v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\rm id}\otimes L_{B}\otimes{\rm id}\otimes{\rm id})({\rm id}\otimes\beta_{G}\otimes{\rm id})({\rm id}\otimes{\rm id}\otimes\rho)(v_{12}v_{13})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\rm id}\otimes L_{B}\otimes{\rm id}\otimes{\rm id})({\rm id}\otimes\beta_{G}\otimes{\rm id})(v_{12}[({\rm id}\otimes\rho)(v)]_{134})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\rm id}\otimes L_{B}\otimes{\rm id}\otimes{\rm id})\bigg([({\rm id}\otimes\rho)(v)]_{123}({\rm id}\otimes\beta\otimes{\rm id})(({\rm id}\otimes\rho)(v))\bigg).

Since
u
is a representation of
H
one has
({\rm id}_{\mathcal{L}(B)}\otimes\Delta)(u)=({\rm id}_{\mathcal{L}(B)}\otimes\Delta_{H})(u)=u_{12}u_{13}
hence,

({\rm id}_{\mathcal{L}(H_{v}\otimes B)}\otimes\Delta)(a(v))=({\rm id}_{\mathcal{L}(H_{v}\otimes B)}\otimes\Delta)[({\rm id}\otimes(L_{B}\otimes{\rm id})\rho)(v)]({\rm id}_{\mathcal{L}(H_{v}\otimes B)}\otimes\Delta)(u_{23})

=({\rm id}\otimes L_{B}\otimes{\rm id}\otimes{\rm id})\bigg([({\rm id}\otimes\rho)(v)]_{123}({\rm id}\otimes\beta\otimes{\rm id})(({\rm id}\otimes\rho)(v))\bigg)u_{23}u_{24}.

On the other hand, viewing
a(v)_{12}a(v)_{13}
as a
4
legged object in
\mathcal{L}(H_{v})\otimes\mathcal{L}(B)\otimes C(\mathbb{G})\otimes C(\mathbb{G})
, and using the relation
u(L_{B}(b)\otimes 1)u^{*}=(L_{B}\otimes{\rm id})(\beta(b))
, we get:

\displaystyle a(v)_{12}a(v)_{13}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\rm id}\otimes L_{B}\otimes{\rm id}\otimes{\rm id})\left([({\rm id}\otimes\rho)(v)]_{123}\right)u_{23}({\rm id}\otimes L_{B}\otimes{\rm id}\otimes{\rm id})\left([({\rm id}\otimes\rho)(v)]_{124}\right)u_{24}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\rm id}\otimes L_{B}\otimes{\rm id}\otimes{\rm id})\bigg([({\rm id}\otimes\rho)(v)]_{123}({\rm id}\otimes\beta\otimes{\rm id})(({\rm id}\otimes\rho)(v))\bigg)u_{23}u_{24}.

This shows that
({\rm id}_{\mathcal{L}(H_{v}\otimes B)}\otimes\Delta)(a(v))=a(v)_{12}a(v)_{13}
,
\forall v\in\mathrm{Rep}(G)
.

We now prove
(6)
. Let
v,w\in\mathrm{Rep}(G)
. Viewing
(\Sigma_{23}\otimes 1)a(v)_{13}a(w)_{23}(\Sigma_{23}\otimes 1)
as a
5
legged object in
\mathcal{L}(H_{v})\otimes\mathcal{L}(H_{w})\otimes\mathcal{L}(B)\otimes\mathcal{L}(B)\otimes C(\mathbb{G})
one has:

(\Sigma_{23}\otimes 1)a(v)_{13}a(w)_{23}(\Sigma_{23}\otimes 1)=[({\rm id}\otimes(L_{B}\otimes{\rm id})\rho)(v)]_{135}u_{35}[({\rm id}\otimes(L_{B}\otimes{\rm id})\rho)(v)]_{245}u_{45}

Hence, for
a,b\in B
, we get, with
Y:=(\Sigma_{23}\otimes 1)a(v)_{13}a(w)_{23}(1\otimes a\otimes 1\otimes b\otimes 1)
,

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[({\rm id}\otimes(L_{B}\otimes{\rm id})\rho)(v)]_{135}u_{35}[({\rm id}\otimes\rho)(v)]_{245}(1\otimes 1\otimes a\otimes\beta(b))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[({\rm id}\otimes(L_{B}\otimes{\rm id})\rho)(v)]_{135}u_{35}(1\otimes 1\otimes a\otimes\beta(b))[({\rm id}\otimes\rho)(v)]_{245}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[({\rm id}\otimes\rho)(v)]_{135}\beta(a)_{35}\beta(b)_{45}[({\rm id}\otimes\rho)(v)]_{245}.

It follows that, for any
S\in\mathrm{Mor}_{G}(v\otimes w,t)
one has:

\displaystyle(S\otimes m\otimes 1)Y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(S\otimes{\rm id}_{B}\otimes 1)[({\rm id}\otimes\rho)(v)]_{134}(1\otimes 1\otimes\beta(a)\beta(b))[({\rm id}\otimes\rho)(w)]_{234}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(S\otimes{\rm id}_{B}\otimes 1)[({\rm id}\otimes\rho)(v)]_{134}[({\rm id}\otimes\rho)(w)]_{234}(1\otimes 1\otimes\beta(ab))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(S\otimes{\rm id}_{B}\otimes 1)({\rm id}\otimes\rho)(v_{13}w_{23})(1\otimes 1\otimes\beta(ab))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\rm id}\otimes\rho)(t)(S\otimes{\rm id}_{B}\otimes 1)(1\otimes 1\otimes\beta(ab))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\rm id}\otimes\rho)(t)(S\otimes{\rm id}_{B}\otimes 1)u_{34}(1\otimes 1\otimes ab\otimes 1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\rm id}\otimes\rho)(t)u_{23}(S\otimes m\otimes 1)(1\otimes 1\otimes a\otimes b\otimes 1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a(t)((S\otimes m)\Sigma_{23})\otimes 1)(1\otimes a\otimes 1\otimes b\otimes 1)

Since this is true for all
a,b\in B
, it follows that

((S\otimes m)\Sigma_{23}\otimes 1)a(v)_{13}a(w)_{23}=a(t)((S\otimes m)\Sigma_{23}\otimes 1).

To show that
(C(\mathbb{G}),\Delta)
is a CQG, we will use the version
[
FP16
, Definition 1.1]
of the definition of a CQG. We already checked the second condition of
[
FP16
, Definition 1.1]
. Let us now check the first condition. Let
\mathcal{X}
be the set of coefficients of
a(v)
, for
v\in{\rm Irr}(G)
. It suffices to show that the unital
*
-subalgebra of
C(\mathbb{G})
generated by
\mathcal{X}
and
{\rm Pol}(H)
is dense in
C(\mathbb{G})
. For
\omega\in B^{*}
define
\omega_{0}\in\mathcal{L}(B)^{*}
,
\omega_{0}(T):=\omega(T1_{B})
. Since
u(1_{B}\otimes 1)=1_{B}\otimes 1
we have,

\displaystyle({\rm id}\otimes\omega_{0}\otimes{\rm id})(a(v))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\rm id}\otimes\omega\otimes{\rm id})\left(({\rm id}\otimes(L_{B}\otimes{\rm id})\rho)(v)u_{23}(1\otimes 1_{B}\otimes 1)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\rm id}\otimes\omega\otimes{\rm id})\left(({\rm id}\otimes(L_{B}\otimes{\rm id})\rho)(v)(1\otimes 1_{B}\otimes 1)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\rm id}\otimes\omega\otimes{\rm id})\left(({\rm id}\otimes\rho)(v)\right)=({\rm id}\otimes(\omega\otimes{\rm id})\rho)(v).

It follows that, for any
v\in\mathrm{Rep}(G)
,
(\omega\otimes{\rm id})\rho(v_{ij})\in\mathcal{X}
, for any coefficient
v_{ij}
of
v
and any
\omega\in B^{*}
. Hence,
\{(\omega\otimes{\rm id})\rho(a)\,:\,a\in{\rm Pol}(G),\,\omega\in B^{*}\}\subset{\rm Span}(\mathcal{X})
. Moreover, since
a(1)=u
,
\mathcal{X}
also contains the coefficients of
u
. So the
*
-algebra generated by
\mathcal{X}
is dense in
C(\mathbb{G})
whenever the action
\beta
is faithful. In general, the
*
-algebra generated by
\mathcal{X}
and
{\rm Pol}(H)
is dense in
C(\mathbb{G})
. It also implies
(5)
, by the general theory of CQG, once we know that
(C(\mathbb{G}),\Delta)
is a CQG. To conclude the proof of
(1)
we only have to check the last condition of
[
FP16
, Definition 1.1]
. To do so, we use
[
FP16
, Remark 1]
. Let, for
v\in\mathrm{Rep}(G)
,
s_{v}\in\mathrm{Mor}_{G}(1,v\otimes\overline{v})
and
s_{\overline{v}}\in\mathrm{Mor}_{G}(1,\overline{v}\otimes v)
be a pair solving the conjugate equation for
(v,\overline{v})
. Since
\eta\in\mathrm{Mor}_{H}(1,u)
,
a(1)=u
, assertion
(6)
gives:

S_{v}:=\Sigma_{23}(s_{v}\otimes m^{\star})\eta\in\mathrm{Mor}(1,a(v)\otimes a(\overline{v}))\quad\text{and}\quad S_{\overline{v}}:=\Sigma_{23}(s_{\overline{v}}\otimes m^{\star})\eta\in\mathrm{Mor}(1,a({\overline{v}})\otimes a(v)).

Suppose that the pair
(s_{v},s_{\overline{v}})
is standard. To show that
(C(\mathbb{G}),\Delta)
is a CQG it remains to prove that
S_{\overline{v}}
is non-degenerated in the sense of
[
FP16
, Remark 1]
. Writing, as in Section
2.2
,
s_{\overline{v}}=\sum_{i}Q_{v,i}^{-\frac{1}{2}}e_{i}^{\overline{v}}\otimes e_{i}^{v}
we find, as in the proof of
[
FP16
, Proposition 2.7]
, and using the notations introduced at the end of Section
2.3
,

S_{\overline{v}}=\sum_{\kappa,i,j,k}\sqrt{\frac{\lambda_{\kappa,j}}{Q_{v,i}\lambda_{\kappa,k}}}e^{\overline{v}}_{i}\otimes b^{\kappa}_{jk}\otimes e^{v}_{i}\otimes b^{\kappa}_{kj}.

From this formula, it is clear that
S_{\overline{v}}
is non-degenerated so
(C(\mathbb{G}),\Delta)
is a CQG. The same computation gives
S_{v}=\sum_{\kappa,i,j,k}\sqrt{\frac{Q_{v,i}\lambda_{\kappa,j}}{\lambda_{\kappa,k}}}e^{v}_{i}\otimes b^{\kappa}_{jk}\otimes e^{\overline{v}}_{i}\otimes b^{\kappa}_{kj}
. From these formulas, one checks easily that the pair
(S_{v},S_{\overline{v}})
solves the conjugate equation for
(a(v),a(\overline{v}))
so
\overline{a(v)}\simeq a(\overline{v})
for all
v\in\mathrm{Rep}(G)
.

Note that statement
(4)
is an easy consequence of the fact that
\rho(f)=1_{B}\otimes f
for all
f\in C(F)
. Finally, the existence and uniqueness of the character
\varepsilon_{\mathbb{G}}
satisfying the properties given in
(2)
is a direct consequence of the universal property of
C(\mathbb{G})
. The fact that it is the counit of
\mathbb{G}
follows from the definition of
\Delta
and
\varepsilon_{\mathbb{G}}
.∎

Definition 3.6
.

The CQG
\mathbb{G}
constructed above is called
the generalized free wreath product
of
G
and
(H,\beta)
, amalgamated on
F
, and denoted by
G\wr_{*,\beta,F}H
. When
F
is the trivial group, we call it the generalized free wreath product of
G
and
(H,\beta)
, denoted
G\wr_{*,\beta}H
.

Recall that the C*-algebra
B
is decomposed as a finite sum of matrix algebras:

B\simeq\bigoplus_{\kappa=1}^{K}M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C}).

We also denote by
1_{\kappa}
the unit of the block
\kappa
so
1_{B}=\sum_{\kappa}1_{\kappa}
.

Remark 3.7
.

The following statements are easy to check.

(1)

If
F
is equal to
G
i.e. the inclusion
C(F)\subset C(G)
is surjective, then
G\wr_{*,\beta,F}H\simeq G\times H
.

(2)

The canonical surjection
\pi_{H}:=({\rm id}\otimes\varepsilon_{G})\pi\,:\,C(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow C(H)
, where
\pi
is constructed in Remark
3.2
intertwines the comultiplications so
H
is a compact quantum subgroup of
\mathbb{G}
. Hence
H
is both a quantum subgroup and a dual quantum subgroup of
\mathbb{G}
.

(3)

There exists a unique unital
*
-homomorphism
\pi_{G}\,:\,C(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow C(G)^{\ast K}
such that, for all
a\in C(G)
,
({\rm id}\otimes\pi_{G})\rho(a)=\sum_{\kappa=1}^{K}1_{\kappa}\otimes a
and
\pi_{G}|_{C(H)}=\varepsilon_{H}(\cdot)1
. Moreover,
\pi_{G}
is easily seen to be surjective and intertwines the comultiplications. Hence
G^{\ast K}
is a compact quantum subgroup of
\mathbb{G}
.

(4)

If
\beta
is the trivial action
B\rightarrow B\otimes C(H)
i.e.
\beta(b)=b\otimes 1
then:

G\wr_{*,\beta,F}H=(G^{\ast_{F}K})\underset{F}{*}(H\times F).

(5)

Suppose that
\beta_{k}\,:\,B_{k}\rightarrow B_{k}\otimes C(H_{k})
,
k=1,2
, two
\psi_{k}
-preserving actions, are
isomorphic
in the sense that there exist two isomorphisms
\pi\,:\,B_{1}\rightarrow B_{2}
and
\varphi\,:\,H_{1}\rightarrow H_{2}
such that
\psi_{2}\circ\pi=\psi_{1}
and
\beta_{2}\circ\pi=(\pi\otimes\varphi)\beta_{1}
. Then,

G\wr_{*,\beta_{1},F}H_{1}\simeq G\wr_{*,\beta_{2},F}H_{2}.

(6)

If we have a pair of dual quantum subgroups of
G
,
\mathbb{C}1_{G}\subset C(F_{1})\subset C(F_{2})\subset C(G)
, then we get surjective unital
*
-homomorphisms:

C(G\wr_{\ast,\beta}H)\rightarrow C(G\wr_{\ast,\beta,F_{1}}H)\rightarrow C(G\wr_{\ast,\beta,F_{2}}H)\rightarrow C(G\wr_{\ast,\beta,G}H)\simeq C(G)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(H),

all of these morphisms intertwining the comultiplications. The last isomorphism is the one from
(1)
, in the case of the amalgamation on
G
itself.

The following Proposition, which can be viewed as a generalization of Remark
3.7

(4)
, shows that, up to an amalgamated free product, the action
\beta
can always be assumed to be faithful.

Proposition 3.8
.

Denoting by
H_{0}
the dual quantum subgroup of
H
generated by the coefficients of
u
, there is an isomorphism
G\wr_{*,\beta,F}H\simeq(G\wr_{*,\beta,F}H_{0})\underset{H_{0}\times F}{\ast}(H\times F)
.

Proof.

Let us denote by
\rho_{0}
the map defining
\mathbb{G}_{0}:=G\wr_{*,\beta,F}H_{0}
,
\rho
the one for
\mathbb{G}:=G\wr_{*,\beta,F}H
and view
C(H_{0}\times F)\subset C(\mathbb{G}_{0})
and
C(H\times F)\subset C(\mathbb{G})
. Define
\Sigma:=\mathbb{G}_{0}\underset{H_{0}\times F}{\ast}(H\times F)
and view
C(\mathbb{G}_{0}),C(H\times F)\subset C(\Sigma)
. By the universal property of
C(\mathbb{G})
, there is a unique unital
*
-homomorphism
\pi\,:\,C(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow C(\Sigma)
such that:

({\rm id}\otimes\pi)\rho=\rho_{0}\,:\,C(G)\rightarrow B\otimes C(\mathbb{G}_{0})\subset B\otimes C(\Sigma)\text{ and }\pi|_{C(H\times F)}\text{ is the inclusion}.

It is easy to check that
\pi
intertwines the comultiplication. Moreover, the universal property of
C(\mathbb{G}_{0})
gives a unique unital
*
-homomorphism
\pi_{0}\,:\,C(\mathbb{G}_{0})\rightarrow C(\mathbb{G})
such that
({\rm id}\otimes\pi_{0})\rho_{0}=\rho
and
\pi_{0}|_{C(H_{0}\times F)}
is the inclusion
C(H_{0}\times F)\subset C(H\times F)\subset C(\mathbb{G})
. Now, by the universal property of the amalgamated free product
C(\Sigma)=C(\mathbb{G}_{0})\underset{C(H_{0}\times F)}{*}C(H\times F)
, there is a unique unital
*
-homomorphism
\pi^{-1}\,:\,C(\Sigma)\rightarrow C(\mathbb{G})
such that
\pi^{-1}|_{C(\mathbb{G}_{0})}=\pi_{0}
and
\pi|_{C(H\times F)}
is the inclusion
C(H\times F)\subset C(\mathbb{G})
. It is clear that
\pi^{-1}
is the inverse of
\pi
.∎

3.3.
Links with previous constructions

In this subsection we explore some more explicit examples and we make links with previously known constructions.

3.3.1.
Generalizations of wreath products

The semi-direct product quantum group is defined and studied in details in
[
Wan19
]
. Let us recall below the basic facts about this construction.

Let
G
be a compact quantum group and
\Lambda
a finite group acting on
C(G)
by automorphisms of
G
, meaning that we have a group homomorphism
\alpha\,:\,\Lambda\rightarrow{\rm Aut}(C(G))
such that
\Delta_{G}\circ\alpha_{g}=(\alpha_{g}\otimes\alpha_{g})\circ\Delta_{G}
for all
g\in\Lambda
. Define the C*-algebra
C(G\rtimes\Lambda)=C(G)\otimes C(\Lambda)
with the comultiplication
\Delta\,:\,C(G\rtimes\Lambda)\rightarrow C(G\rtimes\Lambda)\otimes C(G\rtimes\Lambda)
such that:

\Delta(a\otimes\delta_{r})=\sum_{s\in\Lambda}\left[({\rm id}\otimes\alpha_{s^{-1}})(\Delta_{G}(a))\right]_{13}(1\otimes\delta_{s}\otimes 1\otimes\delta_{s^{-1}r}).

In particular, the inclusion
C(\Lambda)\subset C(G\rtimes\Lambda)\,:\,x\mapsto 1\otimes x
preserves the comultiplications. It is shown in
[
Wan19
]
that the pair
(C(G\rtimes\Lambda),\Delta)
is a compact quantum group (in its maximal version).

Let
\Lambda\curvearrowright X
be any action of a finite group
\Lambda
on a finite set
X
, denoted by
\gamma\cdot x
, for
\gamma\in\Lambda
and
x\in X
. We get an action
\alpha\,:\,\Lambda\rightarrow{\rm Aut}(G^{*_{F}X})
of
\Lambda
on
G^{*_{F}X}
by quantum automorphisms of
G^{*_{F}X}
defined by
\alpha_{\gamma}\circ\nu_{x}=\nu_{\gamma\cdot x}
, where
\nu_{x}\,:\,C(G)\rightarrow C(G^{*_{F}X})=\underset{x\in X,C(F)}{*}C(G)
is the
x^{th}
copy of
C(G)
and we can consider the semi-direct product quantum group
G^{*_{F}X}\rtimes\Lambda
.

Fix a finite dimensional C*-algebra
B
with a faithful state
\omega\in B^{*}
and denote

B^{X}:=B\oplus\dots\oplus B\quad|X|\text{-times}.

Define the faithful state
\psi\in(B^{X})^{*}
by
\psi(b):=\frac{1}{|X|}\sum_{x\in X}\omega(\chi_{x}(b))
, for all
b\in B^{X}
, where
\chi_{x}\,:\,B^{X}\rightarrow B
is the canonical projection on the
x
-copy of
B
in
B^{X}
. Consider now the unique action
\beta\,:\,B^{X}\rightarrow B^{X}\otimes C(\Lambda)
of the quantum group
C(\Lambda)
on the finite dimensional unital C*-algebra
B^{X}
satisfying, for all
x\in X
and
b\in B^{X}
,

(\chi_{x}\otimes{\rm id})\beta(b)=\sum_{\gamma\in\Lambda}\chi_{\gamma^{-1}\cdot x}(b)\otimes\delta_{\gamma}.

Note that
\beta
preserves the faithful state
\psi
on
B^{X}
. In the following Proposition,
K
denotes the number of matrix blocks in the decomposition
B=\bigoplus_{\kappa=1}^{K}M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})
. Consider the matrix units
e^{\kappa}_{ij}\in B
and write
e^{x,\kappa}_{ij}\in B^{X}
for the matrix unit
e^{\kappa}_{ij}\in B
viewed in the
x
-copy of
B
in
B^{X}
. Define
e_{x,\kappa}:=\sum_{r}e^{x,\kappa}_{rr}\in B^{X}
.

Proposition 3.9
.

If
\Lambda\curvearrowright X
is free then there is a canonical isomorphism

G\wr_{*,\beta,F}\Lambda\simeq G^{*_{F}X\times K}\rtimes\Lambda,

where
X\times K:=X\times\{1,\dots,K\}
and the action
\Lambda\curvearrowright X\times K
is given by
\gamma\cdot(x,\kappa):=(\gamma\cdot x,\kappa)
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbb{G}:=G\wr_{*,\beta,F}\Lambda
. Recall that
C(G^{*_{F}X}\rtimes\Lambda)=C(G^{*_{F}X})\otimes C(\Lambda)
and define
\rho\,:\,C(G)\rightarrow B^{X}\otimes C(G^{*_{F}X\times K})\subset B^{X}\otimes C(G^{*_{F}X\times K}\rtimes\Lambda)
by
\rho(a):=\sum_{x\in X,1\leq\kappa\leq K}e_{x,\kappa}\otimes\nu_{x,\kappa}(a)
and both
\iota_{F}\,:\,C(F)\rightarrow C(G^{*_{F}X\times K})\subset C(G^{*_{F}X\times K}\rtimes\Lambda)
and
\iota_{\Lambda}\,:\,C(\Lambda)\rightarrow C(G^{*_{F}X}\rtimes\Lambda)
are the inclusions. It is clear that they satisfy the relations of
C(\mathbb{G})
. Moreover, with these maps,
C(G^{*_{F}X\times F}\rtimes\Lambda)
satisfies the universal property of
C(\mathbb{G})
. Indeed, if
\pi\,:\,C(G)\rightarrow B^{X}\otimes C
,
\iota^{\prime}_{F}\,:\,C(F)\rightarrow C
and
\iota^{\prime}_{\Lambda}\,:\,C(\Lambda)\rightarrow C
are unital
*
-homomorphisms such that

\pi(f)=1_{B^{X}}\otimes\iota^{\prime}_{F}(f),\,\,[\pi(a),({\rm id}\otimes\iota^{\prime}_{\Lambda})(\beta(b))]=0,\,\,[\iota^{\prime}_{\Lambda}(c),\iota^{\prime}_{F}(f)]=0

for all
a\in C(G)
,
b\in B^{X}
,
c\in C(\Lambda)
and
f\in C(F)
. Writing
\pi(a)=\sum_{x,\kappa,i,j}e^{x,\kappa}_{ij}\otimes\pi_{ij}^{x,\kappa}(a)
and applying
(\chi_{x}\otimes{\rm id})
to the relation
[\pi(a),({\rm id}\otimes\iota^{\prime}_{\Lambda})(\beta(b))]=0
we find, for all
x\in X
,
a\in C(G)
and
b\in B^{X}
,

\sum_{\kappa,i,j,\gamma}e^{\kappa}_{ij}\chi_{\gamma^{-1}\cdot x}(b)\otimes\pi^{x,\kappa}_{ij}(a)\iota_{\Lambda}^{\prime}(\delta_{\gamma})=\sum_{\kappa,i,j,\gamma}\chi_{\gamma^{-1}\cdot x}(b)e^{\kappa}_{ij}\otimes\iota_{\Lambda}^{\prime}(\delta_{\gamma})\pi^{x,\kappa}_{ij}(a).

Fix any
\kappa,r,s
and
y\in X
and apply this equation to
b:=e^{\kappa,y}_{rs}\in B^{X}
to get:

\sum_{i}e^{\kappa}_{is}\otimes\pi^{x,\kappa}_{ir}(a)\iota^{\prime}_{\Lambda}(u_{xy})=\sum_{j}e^{\kappa}_{rj}\otimes\iota^{\prime}_{\Lambda}(u_{xy})\pi_{sj}^{x,\kappa}(a),

where
u_{xy}:=1_{\{\gamma\in\Lambda\,:\,\gamma\cdot y=x\}}
. This implies that, for all
\kappa,i,r
, all
a\in C(G)
and all
x,y\in X
,

(2)

\pi^{x,\kappa}_{ir}(a)\iota^{\prime}_{\Lambda}(u_{xy})=\delta_{ir}\iota^{\prime}_{\Lambda}(u_{xy})\pi^{x,\kappa}_{ss}(a).

Since
1=\sum_{y}u_{xy}\in C(\Lambda)
, for all
x\in X
, we deduce in particular that
\pi^{x,\kappa}_{ir}(a)=\delta_{ir}\pi^{x,\kappa}_{ss}(a)
for all
x,\kappa,i,r,s,a
. For
a\in C(G)
let
\pi_{x,\kappa}(a)\in C
be the element satisfying
\pi_{x,\kappa}(a)=\pi^{x,\kappa}_{rr}(a)
for all
1\leq r\leq N_{\kappa}
and note that
\pi
is of the form:

\pi(a)=\sum_{x,\kappa,r}e^{x,\kappa}_{rr}\otimes\pi_{x,\kappa}(a)=\sum_{x,\kappa}e_{x,\kappa}\otimes\pi_{x,\kappa}(a),

Since the projections
e_{x,\kappa}
are pairwise orthogonal and
\pi
is a unital
*
-homomorphism, each
\pi_{x,\kappa}\,:\,C(G)\rightarrow C
is a unital
*
-homomorphism and, since
\pi(f)=1_{B^{X}}\otimes\iota^{\prime}_{F}(f)
, we have
\pi_{x,\kappa}(f)=\iota^{\prime}_{F}(f)
for all
f\in F
. Moreover, Equation
(\ref{EqCummutation})
also implies that, for all
a\in C(G)
and all
x,y\in X
and all
1\leq\kappa\leq K
,
\pi_{x,\kappa}(a)\iota^{\prime}_{\Lambda}(u_{xy})=\iota^{\prime}_{\Lambda}(u_{xy})\pi_{x,\kappa}(a)
. Hence, for all
a\in C(G)
and
(x,\kappa)\in X\times K
,
\pi_{x,\kappa}(a)
commutes with
\iota^{\prime}_{\Lambda}(c)
for all
c\in\mathcal{A}_{x}
, where
\mathcal{A}_{x}\subset C(\Lambda)
is the unital
*
-algebra generated by
\{u_{xy}\,:\,y\in X\}
. Since the action
\Lambda\curvearrowright X
is free,
\mathcal{A}_{x}
separates the points of
\Lambda
and so
\mathcal{A}_{x}=C(\Lambda)
, for all
x\in X
. This implies that for all
a\in A
,
(x,\kappa)\in X\times K
and
c\in C(\Lambda)

[\pi_{x,\kappa}(a),\iota^{\prime}_{\Lambda}(c)]=0
. Hence, there exists a unique unital
*
-homomorphism
\widetilde{\pi}\,:\,C(G^{*_{F}X\times K})\otimes C(\Lambda)\rightarrow C
such that
\widetilde{\pi}\circ\nu_{x,\kappa}=\pi_{x,\kappa}
and
\widetilde{\pi}\circ\iota^{\prime}_{\Lambda}=\iota_{\Lambda}
. This shows the universal property hence we may and will assume that
C(\mathbb{G})=C(G^{*_{F}X\times K})\otimes C(\Lambda)
with maps
\rho
,
\iota_{\Lambda}
and
\iota_{F}
given above. Let us denote by
\Delta_{1}
the comultiplication on
C(\mathbb{G})
given by the free wreath product construction and by
\Delta_{2}
the one given by the semi-direct product construction. It suffices to show that
\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{2}
. Since by construction, they both coincide with
\Delta_{\Lambda}
on
C(\Lambda)
, it suffices to show that
\Delta_{1}\circ\nu_{x,\kappa}=\Delta_{2}\circ\nu_{x,\kappa}
for all
(x,\kappa)\in X\times K
. Note that, for all
a\in{\rm Pol}(G)
,
\Delta_{2}(\nu_{x,\kappa}(a))=\sum_{\gamma\in\Lambda}\nu_{x,\kappa}(a_{(1)})\iota_{\Lambda}(\delta_{\gamma})\otimes\nu_{\gamma^{-1}\cdot x,\kappa}(a_{(2)})
and,

\displaystyle({\rm id}\otimes\Delta_{1})(\rho(a))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{x,\kappa}e_{x,\kappa}\otimes\Delta_{1}(\nu_{x,\kappa}(a))=(\beta_{G}\otimes{\rm id})({\rm id}\otimes\rho)\Delta_{G}(a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\beta_{G}(a_{(1)}\otimes e_{y,\zeta})\otimes\nu_{y,\zeta}(a_{(2)})=\sum\rho(a_{(1)})\beta(e_{y,\zeta})\otimes\nu_{y,\zeta}(a_{(2)})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum e_{x,\kappa}e_{\gamma\cdot y,\zeta}\otimes\nu_{x,\kappa}(a_{(1)})\iota_{\Lambda}(\delta_{\gamma})\otimes\nu_{y,\zeta}(a_{(2)})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum e_{x,\kappa}\otimes\nu_{x,\kappa}(a_{(1)})\iota_{\Lambda}(\delta_{\gamma})\otimes\nu_{\gamma^{-1}\cdot x,\kappa}(a_{(2)})

It follows that
\Delta_{1}\circ\nu_{x,\kappa}=\Delta_{2}\circ\nu_{x,\kappa}
for all
(x,\kappa)\in X\times K
.∎

Remark 3.10
.

Gromada’s wreath product
[
Gro23
]
is a particular case of generalized free wreath products: this follows from Proposition
3.9
(with
B=\mathbb{C}
) and
[
FT24
, Proposition 2.27]
.

3.3.2.
The amalgamated free wreath product by
S_{N}^{+}

This construction is a slight variation on Bichon’s construction by adding an amalgamation on a dual quantum subgroup. This variation on Bichon’s definition is due to Freslon
[
Fre22
]
. The computation of the Haar measure and the study of its operator algebras have been done in
[
FT24
]
. Arguing as in the proof of Proposition
3.1
(and checking that the comultiplications coincide) we can easily prove the following. We leave the proof to the reader.

Proposition 3.11
.

Let
G
be a compact quantum group with a dual quantum subgroup
F
. For the universal action
\beta\,:\,S_{N}^{+}\curvearrowright\mathbb{C}^{N}
there is a canonical isomorphism
G\wr_{*,\beta,F}S_{N}^{+}\simeq G\wr_{*,F}S_{N}^{+}
, where
G\wr_{*,F}S_{N}^{+}
denotes the free wreath product from
[
Fre22
]
.

3.3.3.
The free wreath product by a quantum automorphism group

When
\beta\,:\,{\rm Aut}^{+}(B,\psi)\curvearrowright B
is the universal action of the quantum automorphism group
{\rm Aut}^{+}(B,\psi)
, we identify our generalized free wreath product
G\wr_{*,\beta}{\rm Aut}^{+}(B,\psi)
with the free wreath product
G\wr_{*}{\rm Aut}^{+}(B,\psi)
constructed in
[
FP16
]
in the following Proposition. Let’s begin by recalling the free wreath product construction
G\wr_{*}{\rm Aut}^{+}(B,\psi)
from
[
FP16
]
. The C*-algebra
C(G\wr_{*}{\rm Aut}^{+}(B,\psi))
is the universal unital C*-algebra generated by coefficients of
r(x)\in\mathcal{L}(H_{x}\otimes B)\otimes C(G\wr_{*}{\rm Aut}^{+}(B,\psi))
, for all
x\in{\rm Irr}(G)
, with relations:

•

r(x)
is unitary for all
x\in{\rm Irr}(G)

•

(S\otimes m)\Sigma_{23}\in\mathrm{Mor}(r(x)\otimes r(y),r(z))
for all
S\in\mathrm{Mor}_{G}(x\otimes y,z)
and all
x,y,z\in{\rm Irr}(G)
.

•

\eta\in\mathrm{Mor}(1,r(1))
, where
1
is the trivial representation.

The comultiplication
\Delta\,:\,C(G\wr_{*}{\rm Aut}^{+}(B,\psi))\rightarrow C(G\wr_{*}{\rm Aut}^{+}(B,\psi))\otimes C(G\wr_{*}{\rm Aut}^{+}(B,\psi))
is the unique unital
*
-homomorphism such that
r(x)
is a representation for all
x\in{\rm Irr}(G)
i.e.

({\rm id}\otimes\Delta)(r(x))=r(x)_{12}r(x)_{13}\text{ for all }x\in{\rm Irr}(G).

Proposition 3.12
.

There is a unique
*
-isomorphism
\pi\,:\,C(G\wr_{*}{\rm Aut}^{+}(B,\psi))\rightarrow C(G\wr_{*,\beta}{\rm Aut}^{+}(B,\psi))
such that
({\rm id}\otimes\pi)(r(x))=a(u^{x})\in\mathcal{L}(H_{x})\otimes\mathcal{L}(B)\otimes C(\mathbb{G})
for all
x\in{\rm Irr}(G)
. Moreover,
\pi
intertwines the comultiplications.

Proof.

The uniqueness is clear and the existence of
\pi
is a direct consequence of the universal property of
C(G\wr_{*}{\rm Aut}^{+}(B,\psi))
and assertion
(6)
of Theorem
3.5
. It is clear that
\pi
intertwines the comultiplications since
a(u^{x})
is a unitary representation. Let us construct the inverse of
\pi
. By the universal property of
C(G)
and
[
FP16
, Proposition 8.1]
there exists a unique unital
*
-homomorphism
\varphi_{0}\,:\,C(G)\rightarrow B\otimes C(G\wr_{*}{\rm Aut}^{+}(B,\psi))
such that

({\rm id}\otimes\varphi_{0})(v)=r^{\prime}(v):=r(v)({\rm id}\otimes 1_{B}\otimes 1)\text{ for all }v\in\mathrm{Rep}(G),

where
r(v)
, for non irreducible
v\in\mathrm{Rep}(G)
, is defined just before
[
FP16
, Proposition 8.1]
(denoted there
a(v)
). By the universal property of
C({\rm Aut}^{+}(B,\psi))
there is a unique unital
*
-homomorphism
\iota_{B}\,:\,C({\rm Aut}^{+}(B,\psi))\rightarrow C(G\wr_{*}{\rm Aut}^{+}(B,\psi))
such that
({\rm id}\otimes\iota_{B})(u)=r(1)
. From
[
FP16
, Proposition 8.1]
we know that
({\rm id}_{H_{v}}\otimes m)\in\mathrm{Mor}_{G\wr_{*}{\rm Aut}^{+}(B,\psi)}(r(v)\otimes r(1),r(v))
and
({\rm id}_{H_{v}}\otimes m)\Sigma_{12}\in\mathrm{Mor}_{G\wr_{*}{\rm Aut}^{+}(B,\psi)}(r(1)\otimes r(v),r(v))
. Hence,

\displaystyle({\rm id}_{H_{v}}\otimes m\otimes 1)r(v)_{124}r(1)_{34}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r(v)({\rm id}_{H_{v}}\otimes m\otimes 1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(({\rm id}_{H_{v}}\otimes m)\Sigma_{12}\otimes 1)r(1)_{14}r(v)_{234}(\Sigma_{12}\otimes 1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\rm id}_{H_{v}}\otimes m\otimes 1)r(1)_{24}r(v)_{134}

Take an orthonormal basis
(e_{r})_{r}
of
H_{v}
with matrix units
(e_{rs})_{rs}
in
\mathcal{L}(H_{v})
, write
v=\sum_{r,s}e_{rs}\otimes v_{rs}
and:

r(v)=\sum_{r,s,i,j,\kappa,k,l,\zeta}e_{rs}\otimes b_{ij}^{\kappa}\circ(b_{kl}^{\zeta})^{\star}\otimes r(v)^{ij,\kappa}_{kl,\zeta,rs}\quad r(1)=\sum_{i,j,\kappa,k,l,\zeta}b_{ij}^{\kappa}\circ(b_{kl}^{\zeta})^{\star}\otimes r(1)^{ij,\kappa}_{kl,\zeta,rs}.

Note that
\sum_{\gamma,r}\lambda_{\gamma,r}^{\frac{1}{2}}r(1)^{ij,\kappa}_{rr,\gamma}=\delta_{ij}\lambda_{\kappa,i}^{\frac{1}{2}}
, since
\iota_{B}
is a unital
*
-homomorphism. From the relation
b^{\kappa}_{ip}b^{\zeta}_{qj}=\delta_{\kappa\zeta}\delta_{pq}\lambda^{-\frac{1}{2}}_{\kappa,p}b^{\kappa}_{ij}
we find
m\circ(b^{\kappa}_{ip}\circ(b^{\zeta}_{kl})^{\star}\otimes b^{\kappa^{\prime}}_{qj}\circ(b^{\zeta^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}l^{\prime}})^{\star})=\delta_{\kappa,\kappa^{\prime}}\delta_{p,q}\lambda_{\kappa,p}^{-1/2}b^{\kappa}_{ij}\circ((b^{\zeta}_{kl})^{\star}\otimes(b^{\zeta^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}l^{\prime}})^{\star})
.

It follows that
({\rm id}_{H_{v}}\otimes m\otimes 1)r(v)_{124}r(1)_{34}
is equal to:

\sum e_{rs}\otimes b^{\kappa}_{ij}\circ((b^{\zeta}_{kl})^{\star}\otimes(b^{\zeta^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}l^{\prime}})^{\star})\otimes\left(\sum_{p}\lambda_{\kappa,p}^{-1/2}r(v)^{ip,\kappa}_{kl,\zeta,rs}r(1)^{pj,\kappa}_{k^{\prime}l^{\prime},\zeta^{\prime}}\right),

and
({\rm id}_{H_{v}}\otimes m\otimes 1)r(1)_{24}r(v)_{134}
is equal to:

\sum e_{rs}\otimes b^{\kappa}_{ij}\circ((b^{\zeta}_{kl})^{\star}\otimes(b^{\zeta^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}l^{\prime}})^{\star})\otimes\left(\sum_{p}\lambda_{\kappa,p}^{-1/2}r(1)^{ip,\kappa}_{kl,\zeta}r(v)^{pj,\kappa}_{k^{\prime}l^{\prime},\zeta^{\prime},rs}\right).

Then, for all
r,s,\kappa,i,j,\zeta,k,l,\zeta^{\prime},k^{\prime},l^{\prime}
one has

(3)

\sum_{p}\lambda_{\kappa,p}^{-1/2}r(v)^{ip,\kappa}_{kl,\zeta,rs}r(1)^{pj,\kappa}_{k^{\prime}l^{\prime},\zeta^{\prime}}=\sum_{p}\lambda_{\kappa,p}^{-1/2}r(1)^{ip,\kappa}_{kl,\zeta}r(v)^{pj,\kappa}_{k^{\prime}l^{\prime},\zeta^{\prime},rs}.

Note that
(b_{kl}^{\zeta})^{\star}(1_{B})=\psi((b_{kl}^{\zeta})^{*})=\delta_{kl}\lambda_{\zeta,k}^{1/2}
hence,

r^{\prime}(v)=\sum e_{rs}\otimes b_{ij}^{\kappa}\circ(b_{kl}^{\zeta})^{\star}(1_{B})\otimes r(v)^{ij,\kappa}_{kl,\zeta,rs}=\sum e_{rs}\otimes b_{ij}^{\kappa}\otimes\lambda_{\zeta,k}^{1/2}r(v)^{ij,\kappa}_{kk,\zeta,rs}.

It follows that
\varphi_{0}(v_{rs})=\sum b_{ij}^{\kappa}\otimes\lambda_{\zeta,q}^{1/2}r(v)^{ij,\kappa}_{qq,\zeta,rs}
. Writing
\beta(b^{\alpha}_{kl})=\sum_{\kappa,i,j}b^{\kappa}_{ij}\otimes u^{ij,\kappa}_{kl,\alpha}
and using Equation
(\ref{Eqfwp1})
we deduce that, with
X:=({\rm id}\otimes\iota_{B})(\beta(b^{\alpha}_{kl}))\varphi_{0}(v_{rs})
,

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum b^{\kappa}_{ij}\otimes\sum_{p,\zeta,q}\left(\frac{\lambda_{\zeta,q}}{\lambda_{\kappa,p}}\right)^{1/2}r(1)^{ip,\kappa}_{kl,\alpha}r(v)^{pj,\kappa}_{qq,\zeta,rs}=\sum b^{\kappa}_{ij}\otimes\sum_{p,\zeta,q}\left(\frac{\lambda_{\zeta,q}}{\lambda_{\kappa,p}}\right)^{1/2}r(v)^{ip,\kappa}_{kl,\alpha,rs}r(1)^{pj,\kappa}_{qq,\zeta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum b^{\kappa}_{ij}\otimes\sum_{p}\lambda_{\kappa,p}^{-1/2}r(v)^{ip,\kappa}_{kl,\alpha,rs}\left(\sum_{\zeta,q}\lambda_{\zeta,q}^{1/2}r(1)^{pj,\kappa}_{qq,\zeta}\right)=\sum b^{\kappa}_{ij}\otimes r(v)^{ij,\kappa}_{kl,\alpha,rs}.

The same computation with
Y:=\varphi_{0}(v_{rs})({\rm id}\otimes\iota_{B})(\beta(b^{\alpha}_{kl}))
gives:

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum b^{\kappa}_{ij}\otimes\sum_{p,\zeta,q}\left(\frac{\lambda_{\zeta,q}}{\lambda_{\kappa,p}}\right)^{1/2}r(v)^{ip,\kappa}_{qq,\zeta,rs}r(1)^{pj,\kappa}_{kl,\alpha}=\sum b^{\kappa}_{ij}\otimes\sum_{p,\zeta,q}\left(\frac{\lambda_{\zeta,q}}{\lambda_{\kappa,p}}\right)^{1/2}r(1)^{ip,\kappa}_{qq,\zeta}r(v)^{pj,\kappa}_{kl,\alpha,rs}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum b^{\kappa}_{ij}\otimes\sum_{p}\lambda_{\kappa,p}^{-1/2}\left(\sum_{\zeta,q}\lambda_{\zeta,q}^{1/2}r(1)^{ip,\kappa}_{qq,\zeta}\right)r(v)^{pj,\kappa}_{kl,\alpha,rs}=\sum b^{\kappa}_{ij}\otimes r(v)^{ij,\kappa}_{kl,\alpha,rs}.

Hence,
({\rm id}\otimes\iota_{B})(\beta(b^{\alpha}_{kl}))\varphi_{0}(v_{rs})=\varphi_{0}(v_{rs})({\rm id}\otimes\iota_{B})(\beta(b^{\alpha}_{kl}))
and since this holds for any
v\in\mathrm{Rep}(G)
and any
\zeta,k,l
, we deduce that
[\varphi_{0}(a),({\rm id}\otimes\iota_{B})(\beta(b))]=0
for all
a\in C(G)
and all
b\in B
. By the universal property of
C(G\wr_{*,\beta}{\rm Aut}^{+}(B,\psi))
, there exists a unique unital
*
-homomorphism
\varphi\,:\,C(G\wr_{*,\beta}{\rm Aut}^{+}(B,\psi))\rightarrow C(G\wr_{*}{\rm Aut}^{+}(B,\psi))
such that
\varphi|_{C({\rm Aut}^{+}(B,\psi))}=\iota_{B}
and
({\rm id}\otimes\varphi)\rho=\varphi_{0}
.
∎

3.3.4.
A new example

Even starting from classical groups, our construction of generalized free wreath product produces new CQG, as shown in the following example.

Let
\Lambda
be a finite group and
\Gamma
a discrete group. Consider the
\widehat{\Lambda}
-action on the finite dimensional C*-algebra
B:=C^{*}(\Lambda)
by left translation i.e.
\beta\,:\,C^{*}(\Lambda)\rightarrow C^{*}(\Lambda)\otimes C^{*}(\Lambda)
,
\beta(\gamma)=\gamma\otimes\gamma
for all
\gamma\in\Lambda
and note that
\beta
is
\tau
-preserving, where
\tau
is the canonical trace of
C^{*}(\Lambda)
(i.e. the Haar state of
\widehat{\Lambda}
). As observed in Example
2.18
, this action is ergodic but not
2
-ergodic as soon as
|\Lambda|\neq 2
. Then it is easy to check that
C(\widehat{\Gamma}\wr_{*,\beta}\widehat{\Lambda})
is the universal unital C*-algebra generated by elements
\nu_{\gamma}(g)
, for
\gamma\in\Lambda
and
g\in\Gamma
with relations,

•

(\nu_{\gamma}(g))^{*}=\nu_{\gamma^{-1}}(g^{-1})
and
\nu_{\gamma}(1)=\delta_{\gamma,1}1
for all
g\in\Gamma
and
\gamma\in\Lambda
.

•

\nu_{\gamma}(gh)=\sum_{r,s\in\Lambda,\,rs=\gamma}\nu_{r}(g)\nu_{s}(h)
, for all
g,h\in\Gamma
,
\gamma\in\Lambda
,

•

s\nu_{rs}(g)=\nu_{sr}(g)s
, for all
g\in\Gamma
,
r,s\in\Lambda
,

The comultiplication is:

\Delta(\gamma)=\gamma\otimes\gamma\quad\text{and}\quad\Delta(\nu_{\gamma}(g))=\sum_{r,s\in\Lambda,\,rs=\gamma}\nu_{r}(g)s\otimes\nu_{s}(g)\quad\text{for all }\gamma\in\Lambda,\,g\in\Gamma.

While, to the best of the authors knowledge, this type of quantum groups has never been studied before, the case of an abelian group
\Lambda
is actually well known as shown in the following remark.

Remark 3.13
.

If
\Lambda
is abelian then we have a canonical isomorphism
\widehat{\Gamma}\wr_{*,\beta}\widehat{\Lambda}\simeq\widehat{\Gamma}^{*\Lambda}\rtimes\Lambda
, where the action
\Lambda\curvearrowright\Lambda
is by left multiplication. Indeed, by Proposition
3.9
and Remark
3.7

(5)
, it suffices to show that the two actions
\Lambda\curvearrowright\mathbb{C}^{\Lambda}=C(\Lambda)
and
\widehat{\Lambda}\curvearrowright C^{*}(\Lambda)=C(\widehat{\Lambda})
are isomorphic and it follows from the self Pontryagin’s duality of finite abelian groups.

3.4.
The ergodic decomposition

Whenever the state
\psi
on the C*-algebra
B
is not a
\delta
-form, we can decompose
B
as a direct sum
(B,\psi)=\bigoplus_{i}(B_{i},\psi_{i})
, with
\psi_{i}
a
\delta_{i}
-form for all
i
, for pairwise distinct
\delta_{i}
’s, see Remarks
2.9
and
2.10

(1)
. We will call this decomposition
the ergodic decomposition of
(B,\psi)
.

A result by Brannan demonstrates that the quantum automorphism group of
(B,\psi)
can be decomposed as a free product of the quantum automorphism groups of the blocks
(B_{i},\psi_{i})
. This is expressed through the ergodic decomposition
{\rm Aut}^{+}(B,\psi)\simeq\ast_{i}{\rm Aut}^{+}(B_{i},\psi_{i})
. Fima and Pittau extended this result to the free wreath product in
[
FP16
]
, showing that for any compact quantum group
G
, if we denote
\mathbb{G}_{i}=G\wr_{*}{\rm Aut}^{+}(B_{i},\psi_{i})
, the ergodic decomposition is given by
G\wr_{*}{\rm Aut}^{+}(B,\psi)\simeq\ast_{i}\mathbb{G}_{i}
. However, this result does not generally hold for compact quantum groups acting on
B
via
\psi
-preserving actions. Specifically, when considering compact subgroups of
{\rm Aut}^{+}(B,\psi)
that are quantum automorphism groups of quantum graphs on
B
,
[
Daw24
]
provides an example where the quantum automorphism group of a quantum graph cannot be expressed as a free product of the quantum automorphism groups of its subgraphs. Consequently, we cannot extend the result to generalized free wreath products. Nevertheless, some results remain valid, and our perspective on free wreath products offers new insights into the ergodic decomposition proposed by Fima and Pittau.

We begin by recalling a basic construction of actions.

Proposition 3.14
.

Let
I
be a finite set and for all
i\in I
,
B_{i}
a finite dimensional C*-algebra. Consider the unital C*-algebra
B:=\bigoplus B_{i}
with canonical surjections
\chi_{i}\,:\,B\rightarrow B_{i}
. Suppose that
\beta_{i}\,:\,B_{i}\rightarrow B_{i}\otimes C(H_{i})
is an action, for all
i\in I
. Let
H
be a CQG containing all the
H_{i}
as dual quantum subgroups. Then, there exists a unique action
\beta\,:\,B\rightarrow B\otimes C(H)
such that, viewing
C(H_{i})\subset C(H)
one has, for all
i\in I
,
\beta_{i}\circ\chi_{i}=(\chi_{i}\otimes{\rm id})\beta
. Moreover, if all the
\beta_{i}
are
\psi_{i}
-preserving for some state
\psi\in B_{i}^{*}
then,
\beta
is
\psi
-preserving for
\psi:=|I|^{-1}\sum_{i\in I}\psi_{i}\circ\chi_{i}
.

Proof.

View
\beta_{i}\circ\chi_{i}\,:\,B\rightarrow B_{i}\otimes C(H_{i})\subset B_{i}\otimes C(H)
. By the universal property of direct sum there exists a unique unital
*
-homomorphism
\beta\,:\,B\rightarrow B\otimes C(H)
such that
(\chi_{i}\otimes{\rm id})\beta=\beta_{i}\circ\chi_{i}
. Since
H_{i}
is a dual quantum subgroup of
H
one has, for all
i\in I
,

\displaystyle(\chi_{i}\otimes{\rm id}\otimes{\rm id})({\rm id}\otimes\Delta_{H})\beta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\rm id}\otimes\Delta_{H})\beta_{i}\circ\chi_{i}=({\rm id}\otimes\Delta_{H_{i}})\beta_{i}=(\beta_{i}\otimes{\rm id})\beta_{i}\circ\chi_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\beta_{i}\circ\chi_{i}\otimes{\rm id})\beta=(\chi_{i}\otimes{\rm id}\otimes{\rm id})(\beta\otimes{\rm id})\beta

Hence,
({\rm id}\otimes\Delta_{H})\beta=(\beta\otimes{\rm id})\beta
. Note that
(\chi_{i}\otimes\varepsilon_{H})\beta=({\rm id}\otimes\varepsilon_{H})\beta_{i}\circ\chi_{i}=({\rm id}\otimes\varepsilon_{H_{i}})\beta_{i}\circ\chi_{i}=\chi_{i}(\cdot)1
for all
i\in I
. Hence,
({\rm id}\otimes\varepsilon_{H})\beta={\rm id}_{B}
and
\beta
is an action. The uniqueness and the last statement are clear.∎

The action
\beta\,:\,H\curvearrowright B
constructed in Proposition
3.14
will be called
the action associated with
(\beta_{i})_{i\in I}
.

Example 3.15
.

Let
\beta_{B}\,:\,{\rm Aut}^{+}(B,\psi)\curvearrowright B
be the universal action and assume that
\psi
is not a
\delta
-form. Write the ergodic decomposition decomposition
(B,\psi)=\bigoplus(B_{i},\psi_{i})
. Brannan’s result precisely means that
{\rm Aut}^{+}(B,\psi)=\underset{i\in I}{*}{\rm Aut}^{+}(B_{i},\psi_{i})
and
\beta_{B}
is the action associated with the universal actions
\beta_{B_{i}}\,:\,{\rm Aut}^{+}(B_{i},\psi_{i})\curvearrowright B_{i}
.

Let
H
be the fundamental quantum group of a graph of CQG
(H_{p},\Sigma_{e},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{T})
in the sense of
[
FF14
]
. This means that:

•

\mathcal{G}
is connected graph (in the sense of
[
Ser80
]
) with vertex set
V(\mathcal{G})
, edge set
E(\mathcal{G})
, source and range maps
s,r\,:\,E(\mathcal{G})\rightarrow V(\mathcal{G})
and
\mathcal{T}\subset\mathcal{G}
is a maximal subtree.

•

For every
p\in V(\mathcal{G})
and
e\in E(\mathcal{G})

H_{p}
and
\Sigma_{e}
are CQG.

•

For every
e\in E(\mathcal{G})
,
\Sigma_{e}=\Sigma_{\overline{e}}
.

•

For every
e\in E(\mathcal{G})
,
s_{e}\,:\,C(\Sigma_{e})\rightarrow C(H_{s(e)})
is a faithful unital
*
-homomorphism intertwining the comultiplications (i.e.
\Sigma_{e}
is a dual quantum subgroup of
H_{s(e)}
).

Define
r_{e}:=s_{\overline{e}}\,:\,C(\Sigma_{e})\rightarrow C(H_{r(e)})
.
C(H)
is defined as the universal unital C*-algebra generated by the algebras
C(H_{p})
, for
p\in V(\mathcal{G})
and unitaries
u_{e}
, for
e\in E(\mathcal{G})
with relations:

•

For all
e\in E(\mathcal{G})
,
u_{e}^{*}=u_{\overline{e}}
,

•

For all
e\in E(\mathcal{G})
and all
b\in C(\Sigma_{e})

u_{\overline{e}}s_{e}(b)u_{e}=r_{e}(b)
,

•

u_{e}=1
for all
e\in E(\mathcal{T})
.

By the universal property of
C(H)
, there exists a unique unital
*
-homomorphism
\Delta_{H}\,:\,C(H)\rightarrow C(H)\otimes C(H)
such that
\Delta_{H}(u_{e})=u_{e}\otimes u_{e}
for all
e\in E(\mathcal{G})
and
\Delta_{H}|_{C(H_{p})}=\Delta_{H_{p}}
for all
p\in V(\mathcal{G})
. It is shown in
[
FF14
]
that the pair
H:=(C(H),\Delta_{H})
is a compact quantum group.
H
is called the fundamental quantum group and is denoted by
H=\pi_{1}(H_{p},\Sigma_{e},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{T})
. Note that each
H_{p}
is a dual quantum subgroup of
H
. In what follows we assume moreover that
\mathcal{G}
is a finite graph and we write
s_{e}
for the graph maps defining
H
.

Theorem 3.16
.

For each
p\in V(\mathcal{G})
let
B_{p}
be a finite dimensional C*-algebra with a faithful state
\psi_{p}\in B_{p}^{*}
and
\beta_{p}\,:\,H_{p}\curvearrowright B_{p}
be a
\psi_{p}
-preserving action. Let
(B,\psi):=\bigoplus_{p\in V(\mathcal{G})}(B_{p},\psi_{p})
with the associated
\psi
-preserving action
\beta\,:\,H\curvearrowright B
. Then, for any compact quantum group
G
with dual quantum subgroup
F
, there is a canonical isomorphism

G\wr_{*,\beta,F}H\simeq\pi_{1}(G\wr_{*,\beta_{p},F}H_{p},\Sigma_{e}\times F,\mathcal{G},\mathcal{T}),

where the graph maps on the right are given by

s^{\prime}_{e}:=s_{e}\otimes{\rm id}\,:\,C(\Sigma_{e}\times F)\rightarrow C(H_{s(e)}\times F)\subset C(G\wr_{*,\beta_{s(e)},F}H_{s(e)}),

which clearly intertwines the comultiplications.

Proof.

Write
C(H)=\langle C(H_{p}),u_{e}|p\in V(\mathcal{G}),\,e\in E(\mathcal{G})\rangle
. Let
\mathbb{G}:=G\wr_{*,\beta,F}H
,
\mathbb{G}_{p}:=G\wr_{*,\beta_{p},F}H_{p}
with canonical homomorphisms
\rho\,:\,C(G)\rightarrow B\otimes C(\mathbb{G})
and
\rho_{p}\,:\,C(G)\rightarrow B_{p}\otimes C(\mathbb{G}_{p})
and view
C(H\times F)\subset C(\mathbb{G})
and
C(H_{p}\times F)\subset C(\mathbb{G}_{p})
. Define
\mathbb{G}^{\prime}:=\pi_{1}(\mathbb{G}_{p},\Sigma_{e}\times F,\mathcal{G},\mathcal{T})
and write
C(\mathbb{G}^{\prime})=\langle C(\mathbb{G}_{p}),v_{e}|p\in V(\mathcal{G}),\,e\in E(\mathcal{G})\rangle
. Note that, for all
e\in E(\mathcal{G})
and
b\in C(\Sigma_{e})
one has, viewing
C(\Sigma_{e})\subset C(\Sigma_{e}\times F)
,
r^{\prime}_{e}(b)=v_{\overline{e}}(s^{\prime}_{e}(b))v_{e}
. By the universal property of
C(H)
there exists a unique unital
*
-homomorphism
\nu_{H}\,:\,C(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow C(\mathbb{G}^{\prime})
such that
\nu_{H}(u_{e})=v_{e}
and
\nu_{H}|_{C(H_{p})}
is the inclusion
C(H_{p})\subset C(\mathbb{G}_{p})\subset C(\mathbb{G}^{\prime})
.

Note that, by definition, all the maps
s_{e}^{\prime}|_{C(F)}\,:\,C(F)\subset C(\Sigma_{e}\times F)\rightarrow C(\mathbb{G}_{s(e)})\subset C(\mathbb{G}^{\prime})
coincide, for
e\in E(\mathcal{G})
. Write
\nu_{F}\,:\,C(F)\rightarrow C(\mathbb{G}^{\prime})
the unique map such that
\nu_{F}=s_{e}^{\prime}|_{C(F)}
for all
e\in E(\mathcal{G})
. The fundamental quantum group relations give, for all
f\in C(F)
and
e\in E(\mathcal{G})
,

\nu_{F}(f)=s^{\prime}_{\overline{e}}(f)=r^{\prime}_{e}(f)=v_{\overline{e}}(s^{\prime}_{e}(f))v_{e}=v_{e}^{*}\nu_{F}(f)v_{e}.

Hence,
\nu_{F}(C(F))
commutes with
v_{e}
for all
e\in E(\mathcal{G})
. Also, by the free wreath product relations,
C(F)\subset C(\mathbb{G}_{p})
and
C(H_{p})\subset C(\mathbb{G}_{p})
commute, for all
p\in V(\mathcal{G})
. Hence,
\nu_{F}(C(F))
commutes with the C*-algebra generated by
C(H_{p})
,
p\in V(G)
and
v_{e}
,
e\in E(\mathcal{G})
inside
C(\mathbb{G}^{\prime})
. This C*-algebra is exactly
\nu_{H}(C(H))
so we have the relation
[\nu_{H}(x),\nu_{F}(f)]=0
for all
a\in C(H)
and
f\in C(F)
.

Viewing
C(\mathbb{G}_{p})\subset C(\mathbb{G}^{\prime})
, we may view each map
\rho_{p}
as a map
C(G)\rightarrow B_{p}\otimes C(\mathbb{G}^{\prime})
. By universal property of the direct sum there exists a unique unital
*
-homomorphism
\rho^{\prime}\,:\,C(G)\rightarrow B\otimes C(\mathbb{G}^{\prime})
such that
(\chi_{p}\otimes{\rm id})\rho^{\prime}=\rho_{p}
for all
p\in V(\mathcal{G})
, where
\chi_{p}\,:\,B\rightarrow B_{p}
is the canonical surjection. Note that, for all
p\in V(\mathcal{G})
,

\displaystyle(\chi_{p}\otimes{\rm id})[\rho^{\prime}(a)({\rm id}\otimes\nu_{H})(\beta(b))]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho_{p}(a)({\rm id}\otimes\nu_{H})(\beta_{p}(\chi_{p}(b)))=\rho_{p}(a)\beta_{p}(\chi_{p}(b))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{p}(\chi_{p}(b))\rho_{p}(a)=(\chi_{p}\otimes{\rm id})[({\rm id}\otimes\nu_{H})(\beta(b))\rho^{\prime}(a)].

Hence,
[\rho^{\prime}(a),({\rm id}\otimes\nu_{H})(\beta(b))]=0
for all
a\in C(G)
,
b\in B
. Moreover,

(\chi_{p}\otimes{\rm id})\rho^{\prime}(f)=\rho_{p}(f)=1_{B_{p}}\otimes f\text{ for all }f\in C(F).

Hence,
\rho^{\prime}(f)=1_{B}\otimes f
for all
f\in C(F)
.

By the universal property of
C(\mathbb{G})
, there exists a unique unital
*
-homomorphism

\pi\,:\,C(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow C(\mathbb{G}^{\prime})\text{ such that }({\rm id}\otimes\pi)\rho=\rho^{\prime}\text{ and }\pi|_{C(H)}=\nu_{H},\,\,\pi|_{C(F)}=\nu_{F}.

Consider now the canonical maps:

\beta_{G}\,:\,C(G)\otimes B\rightarrow B\otimes C(\mathbb{G})\text{ and }\beta^{p}_{G}\,:\,C(G)\otimes B_{p}\rightarrow B_{p}\otimes C(\mathbb{G}_{p}).

We claim that:

(4)

(\chi_{p}\otimes\pi)\beta_{G}=\beta_{G}^{p}({\rm id}\otimes\chi_{p})\quad\text{for all }p\in V(\mathcal{G})

Indeed, for
a\in C(G)
and
b\in B
one has,

\displaystyle(\chi_{p}\otimes\pi)\beta_{G}(a\otimes b)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\chi_{p}\otimes\pi)(\rho(a)\beta(b))=(\chi_{p}\otimes{\rm id})(\rho^{\prime}(a))({\rm id}\otimes\pi)(\chi_{p}\otimes{\rm id})(\beta(b))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho_{p}(a)\beta_{p}(\chi_{p}(b))=\beta^{p}_{G}(a\otimes\chi_{p}(b)).

Let us show that
\Delta_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime}}\circ\pi=(\pi\otimes\pi)\circ\Delta_{\mathbb{G}}
. Since
\nu_{H}\,:\,C(H)\rightarrow C(\mathbb{G}^{\prime})
intertwines the comultiplications and
H
is a dual subgroup of
\mathbb{G}
, it suffices to show that

({\rm id}\otimes\Delta_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime}})({\rm id}\otimes\pi)\rho=({\rm id}\otimes\pi\otimes\pi)({\rm id}\otimes\Delta_{\mathbb{G}})\rho.

Using Equation
(\ref{EqBetaG})
we find, for all
p\in V(\mathcal{G})
,

\displaystyle(\chi_{p}\otimes\Delta_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime}})({\rm id}\otimes\pi)\rho

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\chi_{p}\otimes\Delta_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime}})\rho^{\prime}=({\rm id}\otimes\Delta_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime}})\rho_{p}=({\rm id}\otimes\Delta_{\mathbb{G}_{p}})\rho_{p}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\beta_{G}^{p}\otimes{\rm id})({\rm id}\otimes\rho_{p})\Delta_{G}=(\beta_{G}^{p}\otimes{\rm id})({\rm id}\otimes\chi_{p}\otimes{\rm id})({\rm id}\otimes\rho^{\prime})\Delta_{G}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\chi_{p}\otimes\pi\otimes{\rm id})(\beta_{G}\otimes{\rm id})({\rm id}\otimes{\rm id}\otimes\pi)({\rm id}\otimes\rho)\Delta_{G}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\chi_{p}\otimes\pi\otimes\pi)({\rm id}\otimes\Delta_{\mathbb{G}})\rho.

This shows that
\pi
intertwines the comultiplications. It remains to show that
\pi
is invertible. To do so, we construct the inverse of
\pi
. Consider the unital
*
-homomorphism

\rho^{\prime}_{p}:=(\chi_{p}\otimes{\rm id})\rho\,:\,C(G)\rightarrow B_{p}\otimes C(\mathbb{G})

and view
C(H_{p})\subset C(H)\subset C(\mathbb{G})
and
C(F)\subset C(\mathbb{G})
. It is clear that
C(F)
commutes with
C(H_{p})
,
\rho^{\prime}_{p}(f)=1_{B_{p}}\otimes f
for
f\in C(F)
and
[\rho_{p}^{\prime}(a),\beta_{p}(b)]=0
for all
a\in C(G)
and
b\in B_{p}
. Hence there exists a unique unital
*
-homomorphism
\pi_{p}^{-1}\,:\,C(\mathbb{G}_{p})\rightarrow C(\mathbb{G})
such that
({\rm id}\otimes\pi_{p}^{-1})\rho_{p}=\rho_{p}^{\prime}
and
\pi_{p}^{-1}|_{C(H_{p})}
is the inclusion of
C(H_{p})
in
C(\mathbb{G})
. In particular, for each vertex
p\in V(\mathcal{G})
,
\pi_{p}^{-1}|_{C(F)}
is the inclusion of
C(F)
in
C(\mathbb{G})
. Consider the unitaries
u_{e}\in C(H)\subset C(\mathbb{G})
. By the universal property of the fundamental quantum group algebra, there exists a unique morphism
\pi^{-1}\,:\,C(\mathbb{G}^{\prime})\rightarrow C(\mathbb{G})
such that
\pi^{-1}|_{C(\mathbb{G}_{p})}=\pi_{p}^{-1}
and
\pi^{-1}(v_{e})=u_{e}
, for all
p\in V(\mathcal{G})
and
e\in E(\mathcal{G})
. It is now easy to check that
\pi^{-1}
is the inverse of
\pi
.∎

Theorem
3.16
is a generalization of the ergodic decomposition in the following sense.

Remark 3.17
.

When
(B,\psi)=\bigoplus(B_{i},\psi)
is the ergodic decomposition of
(B,\psi)
and
H={\rm Aut}^{+}(B,\psi)
we recover the generalization to the amalgamated case of the ergodic decomposition
[
FP16
, Proposition 5.4]
(by using example
3.15
):

G\wr_{\ast,F}{\rm Aut}^{+}(B,\psi)\simeq\ast_{i\in I,F}\left(G\wr_{\ast,F}{\rm Aut}^{+}(B_{i},\psi_{i})\right).

4.
The block extended C*-algebra and Haar measure

In the entire section, we fix an action
\beta\,:\,B\curvearrowright H
on a finite dimensional C*-algebra
B
with faithful invariant state and we use the notations introduced in Section
2.3
.

4.1.
Block decomposition

Given
1\leq\kappa\leq K
, let
C_{\kappa}(\mathbb{G})\subset C(\mathbb{G})
be the subalgebra generated by
C(H),C(F)
and the coefficients of
\rho_{\kappa}:=(\chi_{\kappa}\otimes{\rm id})\rho\,:\,C(G)\rightarrow M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes C(\mathbb{G})
. Hence,
\rho_{\kappa}(C(G))\subset M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes C_{\kappa}(\mathbb{G})
and the relations
[\rho_{\kappa}(a),\beta_{\kappa}(b)]=0
,
[x,f]=0
and
\rho_{\kappa}(f)=1\otimes f
are satisfied in
M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes C_{\kappa}(\mathbb{G})
for all
a\in C(G)
,
b\in B
,
x\in C(H)
and
f\in C(F)
.
C_{\kappa}(\mathbb{G})
is called a
block subalgebra
. Recall that we view
C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(F)\subset C_{\kappa}(\mathbb{G})
. The block C*-algebra satisfies the following universal property.

Proposition 4.1
.

Fix
1\leq\kappa\leq K
. For any unital C*-algebra
D_{\kappa}
, any unital
*
-morphism
\pi_{\kappa}\,:\,C(G)\rightarrow M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes D_{\kappa}
and any
faithful
unital
*
-morphism
\iota_{\kappa}\,:\,C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(F)\rightarrow D_{\kappa}
such that, for all
a\in C(G)
,
b\in B
,
f\in C(F)
,

\pi_{\kappa}(a)({\rm id}\otimes\iota_{\kappa})(\beta_{\kappa}(b)\otimes 1_{F})=({\rm id}\otimes\iota_{\kappa})(\beta_{\kappa}(b)\otimes 1_{F})\pi_{\kappa}(a)\text{ and }\pi_{\kappa}(f)=1_{\kappa}\otimes\iota_{\kappa}(1\otimes f),

there exists a unique unital
*
-homomorphism
\widetilde{\pi}_{\kappa}\,:\,C_{\kappa}(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow D
such that

({\rm id}\otimes\widetilde{\pi}_{\kappa})\rho_{\kappa}=\pi_{\kappa}\text{ and }\widetilde{\pi}_{\kappa}(xf)=\iota_{\kappa}(x\otimes f)\text{ for all }x\in C(H),f\in C(F).

Proof.

If such
D_{\kappa}
is given with the corresponding
*
-morphisms we define

\mathcal{D}_{0}:=\underset{\gamma\neq\kappa,\,C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(F)}{\ast}C_{\gamma}(\mathbb{G})

and
\mathcal{D}:=D_{\kappa}\underset{C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(F)}{\ast}\mathcal{D}_{0}
, where the amalgamation is with respect to the faithful unital
*
-homomorphisms
\iota_{\kappa}\,:\,C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(F)\rightarrow D_{\kappa}
and the inclusion
C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(F)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{0}
. Composing
\pi_{\kappa}
(resp.
\rho_{\gamma}
) with the inclusion of
D_{\kappa}
(resp.
C_{\gamma}(\mathbb{G})
for
\gamma\neq\kappa
) in
\mathcal{D}
we may view
\pi_{\kappa}\,:\,C(G)\rightarrow M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes\mathcal{D}
(resp.
\rho_{\gamma}\,:\,C(G)\rightarrow M_{N_{\gamma}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes\mathcal{D}
). Use the universal property of the direct sum to get a map
\rho^{\prime}:C(G)\rightarrow B\otimes\mathcal{D}
such that
(\chi_{\gamma}\otimes{\rm id})\rho^{\prime}=\rho_{\gamma}
for
\gamma\neq\kappa
and
(\chi_{\kappa}\otimes{\rm id})\rho^{\prime}=\pi_{\kappa}
, we also have a map
\iota\,:\,C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(F)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
which is the canonical inclusion. Note that, if
a\in C(G)
and
b\in B
, the commutation relation
\rho^{\prime}(a)(({\rm id}\otimes\iota)(\beta(b)\otimes 1_{F}))=(({\rm id}\otimes\iota)(\beta(b)\otimes 1_{F}))\rho^{\prime}(a)
can be checked when composed with any
\chi_{\gamma}\otimes{\rm id}
. For
f\in C(F)
, the relation
\rho^{\prime}(f)=1_{B}\otimes\iota(1\otimes f)
also follows from the fact that we have the relations when applying
(\chi_{\gamma}\otimes{\rm id})
and the fact that we amalgamated the different copies of
C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(F)
.
Applying the universal property of
C(\mathbb{G})
gives a
*
-morphism
\widetilde{\pi}:C(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\mathcal{D},
such that
\rho^{\prime}=({\rm id}\otimes\widetilde{\pi})\circ\rho
and
\widetilde{\pi}_{|C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(F)}=\iota
. Define
\widetilde{\pi}_{\kappa}:=\widetilde{\pi}_{|C_{\kappa}(\mathbb{G})}\,:\,C_{\kappa}(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
. Note that
\pi_{\kappa}=(\chi_{\kappa}\otimes{\rm id})\rho^{\prime}=(\chi_{\kappa}\otimes\widetilde{\pi})\rho=({\rm id}\otimes\widetilde{\pi})\rho_{\kappa}=({\rm id}\otimes\widetilde{\pi}_{\kappa})\rho_{\kappa}
. In particular,
\widetilde{\pi}_{\kappa}(C_{\kappa}(\mathbb{G}))\subset D_{\kappa}
and we may view
\widetilde{\pi}_{\kappa}
as a map from
C_{\kappa}(\mathbb{G})
to
D_{\kappa}
. Hence,
\widetilde{\pi}_{\kappa}|_{C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(F)}=\iota_{\kappa}
and this concludes the proof.∎

Theorem 4.2
.

The following holds.

(1)

There exists a unique unital
*
-isomorphism

\pi\,:\,C_{1}(\mathbb{G})\underset{C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(F)}{*}\dots\underset{C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(F)}{*}C_{K}(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow C(\mathbb{G})

such that, for all
1\leq\kappa\leq K
,
\pi|_{C_{\kappa}(\mathbb{G})}\,:\,C_{\kappa}(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow C(\mathbb{G})
is the inclusion.

(2)

For all
1\leq\kappa\leq K
, there exists a unique unital
*
-isomorphism

\pi_{\kappa}\,:\,(B\otimes C(G))\underset{B\otimes C(F)}{*}(M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(F))\rightarrow M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes C_{\kappa}(\mathbb{G})

such that
\pi_{\kappa}(b\otimes a)=\beta_{\kappa}(b)\rho_{\kappa}(a)
for
b\otimes a\in B\otimes C(G)
and
\pi_{\kappa}|_{M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(F)}
is the inclusion. The amalgamated free product is done with respect to the inclusion map
B\otimes C(F)\rightarrow B\otimes C(G)
, and the map
\beta_{\kappa}\otimes{\rm id}\,:\,B\otimes C(F)\rightarrow M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(F)
. The inverse isomorphism
\varphi_{\kappa}:=\pi_{\kappa}^{-1}
satisfies
\varphi_{\kappa}\circ\rho_{\kappa}(a)=1\otimes a\in B\otimes C(G)
for all
a\in C(G)
and
\varphi_{\kappa}|_{M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(F)}
is the inclusion.

Proof.

(1)
. The existence of
\pi
follows from the universal property of the amalgamated free product. Let us construct the inverse of
\pi
. Denote by
C
the free product of the C*-algebras
C_{\kappa}(\mathbb{G})
, amalgamated over
C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(F)
and view
C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(F)\subset C
. Note that we have
\rho_{\kappa}\,:\,C(G)\rightarrow M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes C_{\kappa}(\mathbb{G})\subset M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes C
such that
\rho_{\kappa}(f)=1_{\kappa}\otimes f
for all
f\in C(F)
and
1\leq\kappa\leq K
. Hence, there exists a unique
\rho^{\prime}\,:\,C(G)\rightarrow B\otimes C
such that
(\chi_{\kappa}\otimes{\rm id})\rho^{\prime}=\rho_{\kappa}
for all
\kappa
and
\rho^{\prime}(f)=1_{B}\otimes f
for all
f\in C(F)
. Since the images of
\rho_{\kappa}
and
\beta_{\kappa}
commute in
M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes C_{\kappa}(\mathbb{G})
for all
\kappa
, it follows that the images of
\rho^{\prime}
and
\beta
commute in
B\otimes C
. By the universal property of
C(\mathbb{G})
, there exists a unique
\varphi\,:\,C(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow C
such that
\varphi|_{C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(F)}
is the inclusion and
({\rm id}\otimes\varphi)\rho=\rho^{\prime}
. It is clear that
\varphi
is the inverse of
\pi
.

(2)
. During this proof we use the notation:

\mathcal{A}_{\kappa}:=(B\otimes C(G))\underset{B\otimes C(F)}{*}(M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(F))

and we write
\nu_{\kappa}\,:\,B\otimes C(G)\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\kappa}
and
\mu_{\kappa}\,:\,M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(F)\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\kappa}
the canonical faithful unital
*
-homomorphisms. They satisfy
\mu_{\kappa}(\beta_{\kappa}(b)\otimes f)=\nu_{\kappa}(b\otimes f)
for all
b\in B
and
f\in C(F)
. We fix once and for all a basis of
M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})
consisting of matrix units
(e^{\kappa}_{ij})_{ij}
.

The existence of
\pi_{\kappa}
is a consequence of the universal property of the amalgamated free product
\mathcal{A}_{\kappa}
. Let us construct its inverse
\varphi_{\kappa}\,:\,M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes C_{\kappa}(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\kappa}
. We define the morphism
\iota\,:\,C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(F)\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\kappa}
,
x\otimes f\mapsto\mu_{\kappa}(1\otimes x\otimes f)
and we consider the elements

\varphi^{\kappa}_{ij}(a):=\sum_{s=1}^{N_{\kappa}}\mu_{\kappa}(e^{\kappa}_{si}\otimes 1\otimes 1)\nu_{\kappa}(1\otimes a)\mu_{\kappa}(e^{\kappa}_{js}\otimes 1\otimes 1)\in\mathcal{A}_{\kappa}\text{ for }a\in C(G)\text{ and }1\leq i,j\leq N_{\kappa}.

A direct computation gives the relations:

(i)

\varphi^{\kappa}_{ij}(ab)=\sum_{k}\varphi^{\kappa}_{ik}(a)\varphi^{\kappa}_{kj}(b)
.

(ii)

(\varphi^{\kappa}_{ij}(a))^{*}=\varphi^{\kappa}_{ji}(a^{*})
.

(iii)

\varphi^{\kappa}_{ij}(1)=\delta_{ij}1_{\mathcal{A}_{\kappa}}
.

(iv)

\varphi^{\kappa}_{ij}(f)=\delta_{ij}\iota(1\otimes f)
if
f\in C(F)
.

Note that
(ii)
,
(iii)
and
(iv)
are clear so we only prove
(i)
. Let
a,b\in C(G)
then,

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\kappa}}\varphi^{\kappa}_{ik}(a)\varphi^{\kappa}_{kj}(b)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k,s}\mu_{\kappa}(e^{\kappa}_{si}\otimes 1\otimes 1)\nu_{\kappa}(1\otimes a)\mu_{\kappa}(e^{\kappa}_{kk}\otimes 1\otimes 1)\nu_{\kappa}(1\otimes b)\mu_{\kappa}(e^{\kappa}_{js}\otimes 1\otimes 1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{s}\mu_{\kappa}(e^{\kappa}_{si}\otimes 1\otimes 1)\nu_{\kappa}(1\otimes ab)\mu_{\kappa}(e^{\kappa}_{js}\otimes 1\otimes 1)=\varphi^{\kappa}_{ij}(ab).

This relations mean that
\varphi_{\kappa}\,:\,C(G)\rightarrow M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes\mathcal{A}_{\kappa}
defined by
\varphi_{\kappa}(a):=\sum_{i,j=1}^{N_{\kappa}}e^{\kappa}_{ij}\otimes\varphi^{\kappa}_{ij}(a)
is a unital
*
-homomorphism. Let us check that
\varphi_{\kappa}(a)
and
({\rm id}\otimes\iota)(\beta_{\kappa}(b)\otimes 1)
commutes, for all
a\in C(G)
and
b\in B
. We write
\beta_{\kappa}\,:\,B\rightarrow M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes C(H)
with coefficients
\beta_{\kappa}(b)=\sum_{i,j}e^{\kappa}_{ij}\otimes\beta^{\kappa}_{ij}(b)
. Then, for all
b\in B
,

(e^{\kappa}_{si}\otimes 1)\beta_{\kappa}(b)=\sum_{r}e^{\kappa}_{sr}\otimes\beta^{\kappa}_{ir}(b)\quad\text{and}\quad\beta_{\kappa}(b)(e^{\kappa}_{js}\otimes 1)=\sum_{r}e^{\kappa}_{rs}\otimes\beta^{\kappa}_{rj}(b).

Hence, writing
({\rm id}\otimes\iota)(\beta_{\kappa}(b)\otimes 1)\varphi_{\kappa}(a)=\sum_{i,j}e^{\kappa}_{ij}\otimes X^{\kappa}_{ij}
,

\displaystyle X^{\kappa}_{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{r,s}\iota(\beta^{\kappa}_{ir}(b)\otimes 1)\varphi^{\kappa}_{rj}(a)=\sum_{r,s}\mu_{\kappa}(e^{\kappa}_{sr}\otimes\beta^{\kappa}_{ir}(b)\otimes 1)\nu_{\kappa}(1\otimes a)\mu_{\kappa}(e^{\kappa}_{js}\otimes 1\otimes 1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{s}\mu_{\kappa}((e^{\kappa}_{si}\otimes 1)\beta_{\kappa}(b)\otimes 1)\nu_{\kappa}(1\otimes a)\mu_{\kappa}(e^{\kappa}_{js}\otimes 1\otimes 1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{s}\mu_{\kappa}(e^{\kappa}_{si}\otimes 1\otimes 1)\nu_{\kappa}(b\otimes a)\mu_{\kappa}(e^{\kappa}_{js}\otimes 1\otimes 1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{s}\mu_{\kappa}((e^{\kappa}_{si}\otimes 1\otimes 1)\nu_{\kappa}(1\otimes a)\mu_{\kappa}(\beta_{\kappa}(b)(e^{\kappa}_{js}\otimes 1)\otimes 1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{r,s}\mu_{\kappa}(e^{\kappa}_{si}\otimes 1\otimes 1)\nu_{\kappa}(1\otimes a)\mu_{\kappa}(e^{\kappa}_{rs}\otimes\beta^{\kappa}_{rj}(b)\otimes 1)=\sum_{r}\varphi^{\kappa}_{ir}(a)\iota(\beta^{\kappa}_{rj}(b)\otimes 1).

This shows that
({\rm id}\otimes\iota)(\beta_{\kappa}(b)\otimes 1)\varphi_{\kappa}(a)=\varphi_{\kappa}(a)({\rm id}\otimes\iota)(\beta_{\kappa}(b)\otimes 1)
for all
a\in C(G)
and
b\in B
. Note that, for all
f\in C(F)
,
\varphi_{\kappa}(f)=\sum_{i,j}e_{ij}^{\kappa}\otimes\delta_{ij}\iota(1\otimes f)=1_{\kappa}\otimes\iota(1\otimes f)
. The universal property of
C_{\kappa}(\mathbb{G})
(Proposition
4.1
) gives a unital
*
-homomorphism, still denoted
\varphi_{\kappa}\,:\,C_{\kappa}(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\kappa}
such that
\varphi_{\kappa}(\rho^{\kappa}_{ij}(a))=\varphi^{\kappa}_{ij}(a)
for all
1\leq i,j\leq N_{\kappa}
and all
a\in C(G)
and
\varphi_{\kappa}(x\otimes f)=\mu_{\kappa}(1\otimes x\otimes f)
for all
x\in C(H)
and
f\in C(F)
. Now, we note that
\varphi_{\kappa}(C_{\kappa}(\mathbb{G}))\subseteq\mu_{\kappa}(M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes 1\otimes 1)^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\kappa}
. Indeed, it is clear that, for all
x\in C(H)
and
f\in C(F)
,
\varphi_{\kappa}(x\otimes f)=\mu_{\kappa}(1\otimes x\otimes f)
commutes with
\mu_{\kappa}(M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes 1\otimes 1)
and, if
x=\rho^{\kappa}_{ij}(a)
is a coefficient of
\rho_{\kappa}
, then:

\displaystyle\varphi_{\kappa}(x)\mu_{\kappa}(e^{\kappa}_{rs}\otimes 1\otimes 1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{t}\mu_{\kappa}(e^{\kappa}_{ti}\otimes 1\otimes 1)\nu_{\kappa}(1\otimes a)\mu_{\kappa}(e^{\kappa}_{jt}e^{\kappa}_{rs}\otimes 1\otimes 1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu_{\kappa}(e^{\kappa}_{ri}\otimes 1\otimes 1)\nu_{\kappa}(1\otimes a)\mu_{\kappa}(e^{\kappa}_{js}\otimes 1\otimes 1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu_{\kappa}(e^{\kappa}_{rs}\otimes 1\otimes 1)\varphi_{\kappa}(x).

Hence,
\varphi_{\kappa}(x)
commutes with
\mu_{\kappa}(M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes 1\otimes 1)
for any
x
which is a coefficients of
\rho_{\kappa}
. Since
C_{\kappa}(\mathbb{G})
is generated by
C(H)
and coefficients of
\rho_{\kappa}
, it follows that

\varphi_{\kappa}(C_{\kappa}(\mathbb{G}))\subseteq\mu_{\kappa}(M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes 1\otimes 1)^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\kappa}.

Hence, there exists a unique unital
*
-homomorphism, still denoted
\varphi_{\kappa}\,:\,M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes C_{\kappa}(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\kappa}
such that
\varphi_{\kappa}(b\otimes x)=\mu_{\kappa}(b\otimes 1\otimes 1)\varphi_{\kappa}(x)
, for all
b\in M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})
and
x\in C_{\kappa}(\mathbb{G})
. By construction we have
\varphi_{\kappa}\circ\rho_{\kappa}(a)=\nu_{\kappa}(1\otimes a)
for all
a\in C(G)
and
\varphi_{\kappa}|_{M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(F)}=\mu_{\kappa}
. Since
M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes C_{\kappa}(\mathbb{G})
is generated, as a C*-algebra, by
\rho_{\kappa}(C(G))\cup M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(F)
this easily implies that
\varphi_{\kappa}
is the inverse of
\pi_{\kappa}
.∎

4.2.
Reduced operators

From now on we suppose that the action
\beta\,:\,H\curvearrowright B
is ergodic with unique invariant state
\psi\in B^{*}
and associated representation
u\in\mathcal{L}(B)\otimes C(H)
. Let
h_{H}
be the Haar measure of
H
.

Let
G
be a compact quantum group with a dual quantum subgroup
F
and associated ucp map
E_{F}:C(G)\rightarrow C(F)
. For all
a\in{\rm Pol}(G)
, we may write
\Delta(a)=\sum a_{(1)}\otimes a_{(2)}
with
a_{(1)},a_{(2)}\in{\rm Pol}(G)
. If moreover
E_{F}(a)=0
then we may choose
a_{(1)}
and
a_{(2)}
such that
E_{F}(a_{(1)})=E_{F}(a_{(2)})=0
for all
(1)
,
(2)
(by Remark
2.6
).

Let
\mathbb{G}=G\wr_{*,\beta,F}H
the amalgamated free wreath product quantum group with generic morphism
\rho\,:\,C(G)\rightarrow B\otimes C(\mathbb{G})
and
C_{\kappa}(\mathbb{G})\subseteq C(\mathbb{G})
be a block algebra.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}\subset M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes C_{\kappa}(\mathbb{G})
be the linear span of
reduced operators
in
M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes C_{\kappa}(\mathbb{G})
i.e. operators
x\in M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes C_{\kappa}(\mathbb{G})
which can be written as products alternating from operators of the form
X\in M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes{\rm Pol}(H)\otimes{\rm Pol}(F)\subset M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes C_{\kappa}(\mathbb{G})
with
(E_{\kappa}\otimes{\rm id}_{F})(X)=0
and operators of the form
\rho_{\kappa}(a):=(\chi_{\kappa}\otimes{\rm id})\rho(a)
, with
a\in{\rm Pol}(G)
and
E_{F}(a)=0
. More precisely,
x
is of the form

•

x=X_{1}\rho_{\kappa}(a_{1})\dots X_{n}\rho_{\kappa}(a_{n})
or,

•

x=\rho_{\kappa}(a_{1})X_{1}\dots\rho_{\kappa}(a_{n})X_{n}
or,

•

x=X_{0}\rho_{\kappa}(a_{1})X_{1}\dots\rho_{\kappa}(a_{n})X_{n}
or,

•

x=\rho_{\kappa}(a_{0})X_{1}\rho_{\kappa}(a_{1})\dots X_{n}\rho_{\kappa}(a_{n})
or,

•

x=\rho_{\kappa}(a_{1})(\beta_{\kappa}(b)\otimes 1_{C(F)})
for some
b\in B
.

where
n\geq 1
,
X_{s}\in M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes{\rm Pol}(H)\otimes{\rm Pol}(F)
,
a_{s}\in{\rm Pol}(G)
,
E_{F}(a_{s})=0
,
(E_{\kappa}\otimes{\rm id}_{F})(X_{s})=0
.

Lemma 4.3
.

The following holds.

(1)

X_{\kappa}:=\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}+M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes{\rm Pol}(H)\otimes{\rm Pol}(F)
is a dense subalgebra of
M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes C_{\kappa}(\mathbb{G})
.

(2)

There exists a unique conditional expectation

\mathbb{E}_{\kappa}\,:\,M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes C_{\kappa}(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(F)\text{ such that }\ker(\mathbb{E}_{\kappa})=\overline{\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}}.

(3)

\Delta(C_{\kappa}(\mathbb{G}))\subset C_{\kappa}(\mathbb{G})\otimes C(\mathbb{G})
and
({\rm id}_{M_{N_{\kappa}}}\otimes\Delta)(\mathcal{C}_{\kappa})\subset\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}\otimes C(\mathbb{G})
.

(4)

For all
X\in M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes C_{\kappa}(\mathbb{G})
one has:

(\mathbb{E}_{\kappa}\otimes{\rm id}_{C(\mathbb{G})})({\rm id}_{M_{N_{\kappa}}}\otimes\Delta)(X)=({\rm id}_{M_{N_{\kappa}}}\otimes\Delta)\mathbb{E}_{\kappa}(X).

(5)

For all
X\in M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes C_{\kappa}(\mathbb{G})
one has:

(({\rm id}_{M_{N_{\kappa}}}\otimes h_{H}\otimes h_{F})\circ\mathbb{E}_{\kappa}\otimes{\rm id}_{C(\mathbb{G})})({\rm id}_{M_{N_{\kappa}}}\otimes\Delta)(X)=({\rm id}_{M_{N_{\kappa}}}\otimes h_{H}\otimes h_{F})\circ\mathbb{E}_{\kappa}(X)\otimes 1_{C(\mathbb{G})}.

Proof.

(1)
. It is easy to see that
X_{\kappa}
is a unital
*
-subalgebra of
M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes C_{\kappa}(\mathbb{G})
containing
M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes{\rm Pol}(H)\otimes{\rm Pol}(F)
. Note that it also contains
1\otimes\rho^{\kappa}_{ij}(a)
for all
a\in{\rm Pol}(G)
. Indeed, writing
1\otimes\rho^{\kappa}_{ij}(a)=\sum_{r}(e^{\kappa}_{ri}\otimes 1)\rho_{\kappa}(a)(e^{\kappa}_{jr}\otimes 1)
and
e^{\kappa}_{rs}=(e^{\kappa}_{rs})^{\circ}+\frac{\delta_{rs}}{\delta\lambda_{\kappa,r}}1_{B}
, where
E_{\kappa}((e^{\kappa}_{rs})^{\circ}\otimes 1)=0
we see that
1\otimes\rho^{\kappa}_{ij}(a)\in X
when
E_{F}(a)=0
and, for any
a\in{\rm Pol}(G)
, writing
a=a^{\circ}+E_{F}(a)
we have
\rho_{ij}^{\kappa}(a)=\rho^{\kappa}_{ij}(a^{\circ})+\rho^{\kappa}_{ij}(E_{F}(a))=\rho^{\kappa}_{ij}(a^{\circ})+\delta_{ij}(1\otimes 1\otimes E_{F}(a))
which implies that
1\otimes\rho^{\kappa}_{ij}(a)\in X
.

(2)
. As in the proof of Theorem
4.2
, we write

\mathcal{A}_{\kappa}:=(B\otimes C(G))\underset{B\otimes C(F)}{*}(M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(F)),

where the amalgamation is with respect to the inclusion
B\otimes C(F)\rightarrow B\otimes C(G)
and the unital
*
-homomorphism
\beta_{\kappa}\otimes{\rm id}_{F}\,:\,B\otimes C(F)\rightarrow M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(F)
and we write
\nu_{\kappa}\,:\,B\otimes C(G)\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\kappa}
and
\mu_{\kappa}\,:\,M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(F)\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\kappa}
the canonical faithful unital
*
-homomorphism. Consider the unique ucp map
E_{2}\,:\,\mathcal{A}_{\kappa}\rightarrow M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(F)
such that
E_{2}\circ\mu_{\kappa}={\rm id}
,
E_{2}\circ\nu_{\kappa}=\beta_{\kappa}\otimes E_{F}
and
E_{2}(x)=0
for all
x
reduced of length
\geq 2
in
\mathcal{A}_{\kappa}
in the amalgamated free product sense and with respect to the ucp maps
({\rm id}\otimes E_{F})\,:\,B\otimes C(G)\rightarrow B\otimes C(F)
and

E_{\kappa}\otimes{\rm id}_{F}\,:\,M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes C(H)\otimes_{{\rm max}}C(F)\rightarrow B\otimes C(F).

Recall that
\varphi_{\kappa}:=\pi_{\kappa}^{-1}\,:\,M_{N_{\kappa}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes C_{\kappa}(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\kappa}
is constructed in Theorem
